NASA-funded mission comes to a premature end due to glitch in fault protection system. Read More … Read More

Agility Robotics, which makes the Digit humanoid robot for warehouses and factories, began trading on the Nasdaq on Thursday after a $2.5 billion SPAC merger. Co-founder Jonathan Hurst described how Amazon’s warehouse tests convinced the company it needed a robot that could work safely next to people. Read More … Read More

Shield AI agreed to acquire Echodyne, a maker of compact radar that detects and tracks drones, in a deal valued at up to $5.3 billion. Shield is raising $2.5 billion to pay for it, including $1 billion in new funding at a $28.8 billion valuation, more than double its value in March. Read More … Read More

After more than 33 years at Microsoft, CVP Mazhar Mohammed has left for AWS, while the Redmond, Wash., company has gained a new CVP in its Agent Cloud group. Seattle’s Lila Bio has appointed a chief development officer; the credit union BECU named an AI leader and other personnel changes. Read More … Read More

After his mother-in-law was hurt in a fall and told no one, ex-Microsoft exec Michael Nassirian built a camera-free way for families to know when something’s off. Read More … Read More

In just a few weeks, AWS re:Invent brings advanced and expert-level sessions on the services you build on every day, from serverless and containers to databases and developer tools. Go hands-on with new launches the same week they’re announced, and hear directly from the engineers who built them. Experience the depth you won’t find anywhere else.

Freddy Adu reflects on his Blackpool stint

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

The American trained with the Seasiders in 2014

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

After being dubbed the ‘new Pele’ at the tender age of 14, Freddy Adu’s career would end up taking him to some unexpected places.

After becoming the youngest-ever player to appear in United States professional sports when he made his D.C. United debut in 2004, Adu had stints playing in Portugal, France, Greece, Turkey and Brazil over the next decade.

After a stint with Salvador-based Brazilian side Bahia ended, Adu’s next opportunity came at Blackpool, with the then-Championship side taking the American on trial in February 2014.

And while moving from the Brazilian summer to a Blackpool winter would be a culture shock to most, Adu looks back at his time with the Seasiders fondly.

“I had a good time there, it was great!” he tells FourFourTwo. “I was a free agent after Brazil, and Blackpool was cool!”

“I liked it there, I remember I really did enjoy my time there, being around those guys, but the football was really different, compared to what I was used to.”

Adu’s nomadic career to this point had seen him adapt to plenty of new surroundings, but he admits that the rough-and-tumble style of a second-tier English side was something new to him.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

“It was more direct and very physically demanding, so my style of play didn’t exactly mesh with the team,” he admits.

Blackpool would decide against offering Adu a contract, but would allow him to continue training with the club as Adu looked for his next opportunity.

After stints training in Norway and the Netherlands, he would sign with Serbian side FK Jagodina in July 2014, in a move that again didn’t work out for him.

“I went to Serbia and just played the one game, I was only there for about six months,” Adu continues. “At that point in my career, I just needed to try to get somewhere, get some playing time and get my love for soccer back again. It didn’t end up happening over there, I wasn’t happy there.

“They sell you this story but you get there and things are completely different, so I was just trying to bide my time and get out of there.

"Hindsight isn’t 20/20, but there are a lot of decisions I would have made differently, as far as teams go, if I had that opportunity.”

For more than a decade, Joe Mewis has worked in football journalism as a reporter and editor. Mewis has had stints at Mirror Football and LeedsLive among others and worked at FourFourTwo throughout Euro 2024, reporting on the tournament. In addition to his journalist work, Mewis is also the author of four football history books that include times on Leeds United and the England national team. Now working as a digital marketing coordinator at Harrogate Town, too, Mewis counts some of his best career moments as being in the iconic Spygate press conference under Marcelo Bielsa and seeing his beloved Leeds lift the Championship trophy during lockdown.

Legendary sci-fi magazine 'Starlog' returns as a yearly release, just in time for its 50th anniversary

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Before the rise of online entertainment sites, this is where genre fans got their news

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

"Starlog" was one of the greatest sci-fi and fantasy magazines ever, and after a 17-year absence, it's being resurrected.

The legendary sci-fi mag went dark back in 2009 as part of the wave of popular publications folding due to years of declining readership and subscriptions. But as of Nov. 13, this geek favorite periodical returns as an annual print edition, and it's available for pre-order now.

It’s another indication of digital fatigue in the world, and fans of a certain age will recall the pleasure of poring over each colorful issue packed with in-depth interviews, behind-the-scenes photos, TV guides, classic movie retrospectives, book reviews, con news, and VFX features.

"Starlog" launched in 1976, in the pre-"Star Wars" days, under the leadership of editor-in-chief Kerry O’Quinn. It first arrived with artist Jack Thurston's beautiful painted "Star Trek" cover, featuring William Shatner as Captain James T. Kirk and Leonard Nimoy’s Spock, set against the U.S.S. Enterprise near a shimmering black hole.

The magazine continued to inform the science fiction community about upcoming sci-fi TV shows and blockbuster Hollywood films for over three decades; I fondly recall reading each informative and entertaining monthly issue cover-to-cover.

Just in time to honor its 50th anniversary this year, "Starlog" blasts back into the sci-fi universe with Issue #375 showcasing cover artwork created by Armenian artist Gabriel Yeganyan. Its geek culture legacy, covering iconic franchises like "Star Wars," "Star Trek," and "Alien," will now live on thanks to Fangoria Holdings and io9 co-founder Annalee Newitz, editor-in-chief.

"During its original 33-year run, 'Starlog' documented science fiction as it evolved across film, television, books, technology, and culture," said 'Fangoria's Tara Ansley.

"Fifty years after the magazine first launched, the line between science fiction and the real world has become increasingly difficult to separate. That makes this an especially interesting time to bring 'Starlog' back. Technology may change, but the most compelling questions have always been about people: how we live, what we create, and what kind of future we want to build."

"Starlog" reloads for its first annual edition after a long hiatus on Nov. 13. You can pre-order the first issue (Issue 375 in continuity) on the Starlog website.

Jeff Spry is an award-winning screenwriter and veteran freelance journalist covering TV, movies, video games, books, and comics. His work has appeared at SYFY Wire, Inverse, Collider, Bleeding Cool and elsewhere. Jeff lives in beautiful Bend, Oregon amid the ponderosa pines, classic muscle cars, a crypt of collector horror comics, and two loyal English Setters.

The Xbox Game Pass Perks program is continuing to add a bunch of freebies for Game Pass members throughout November 2026, including for the likes of Skate, Sea of Thieves and much more!

Fortnite Crew and the Rocket League Rocket Pass are notable ones that are only available with Xbox Game Pass Ultimate.

To claim your free Xbox Perks on console, you'll need to navigate to the Xbox Game Pass app and look for the 'My Benefits' button.

To claim your Xbox Perks on PC, you'll need to open up the Xbox PC app, hit 'Game Pass' and then choose "Benefits".

The exception to the rule is for some of the free-to-play games where you'll find the Perks automatically included when you load them up. If you look for the "Recently Added" section in the Xbox Game Pass app on console, any free-to-play title in there should grant you a special Perk when you start up those games.

How To Combine World Of Hyatt Points Between Accounts For Free

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

The major hotel loyalty programs all have different policies when it comes to combining points between accounts belonging to different members. I’ve already covered the options and policies for Hilton Honors and Marriott Bonvoy, and in this post, I’d like to take a look at the policy of World of Hyatt.

The good news is that World of Hyatt recently introduced a new way to transfer points to others for free, and I’d argue the program now has among the most generous policies in the industry. Long story short, you can transfer or receive up to 525,000 points per year, and this can all be done online, and at no cost. In this post, I’d like to look more closely at the mechanics of Hyatt points transfers.

World of Hyatt offers a generous feature to allow members to transfer points to others at no cost. Let’s first cover the basic rules for Hyatt points transfers:

It’s worth noting that this system is much better than it used to be. Back in the day combining Hyatt points required completing a form and took days, and you could only combine points in order to have sufficient points for a specific redemption. That policy no longer applies.

The process of transferring World of Hyatt points between members online is straightforward. To start, you can either follow this link, or go to your account overview page, and go to the section that reads “Gift your points more seamlessly.”

On the next page, you’ll want to go to the “Gift points or eligible awards” section, and then click “Gift your points.”

There you’ll first want to select how many points you want to transfer, using the slider.

Then on the next page, you’ll need to enter the account information for the account you want to move points to.

Then you’ll just need to confirm the details, and that’s it! Regarding transfer times, reports suggest transfers take anywhere from a few minutes to several days. It does seem like the more points you transfer, the more likely that it takes days for the transfer to go through. So if you are moving a large chunk of points, don’t be surprised if it takes some time.

What are the benefits to being able to combine points between World of Hyatt accounts? On the most basic level, it allows you to combine points so you have enough for an award stay. In other words, if you have two accounts with 15,000 points each, you can combine the points so you have enough for a one night stay at a hotel costing 30,000 points.

Furthermore, if you’re traveling as multiple people and one person has elite status, there’s value to booking the stay out of their account, so that you could take advantage of World of Hyatt Globalist or World of Hyatt Explorist benefits, for example.

However, thanks to the Guest of Honor program, it’s actually pretty easy to give elite perks to friends and family nowadays. For that matter, even Suite Upgrade Awards and Club Access Awards are transferable nowadays.

Lastly, combining points is also considered “activity” for the purposes of keeping your account alive, so it would reset the expiration of points.

World of Hyatt has a great system for letting members combine and share points. World of Hyatt members can transfer up to 525,000 points per year to other members, with limited restrictions. This is possible online, and typically points transfers happen pretty quickly.

There’s lots of value to this, and I’d say Hyatt’s policy is comparable to Hilton Honors for being the best in the industry.

If you’ve ever combined World of Hyatt points, what was your experience like?

Membership is free and takes no more information than commenting as a guest. Earn status points for good contributions, and see fewer ads as your status grows.

Report: John Ternus pushing Craig Federighi to speed up Apple software engineering

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

In the past few years, a major Apple story (especially in the age of AI) has been that the company isn’t moving fast enough in the space to keep up with competitors. According to Bloomberg’s Mark Gurman, new Apple CEO John Ternus is reportedly pushing on software chief Craig Federighi to ‘accelerate’ Apple’s roadmap for software.

At the moment, there’s no indication that Apple’s well known software SVP Craig Federighi will be leaving the company. In fact, Craig has made it a very low priority of his to develop a succession plan in any sense, per Mark Gurman. That said, Ternus still wants to see change in how Craig Federighi thinks about Apple’s software strategy:

In recent weeks, Ternus held a closed-door meeting with Craig Federighi, who became Apple’s AI czar following last year’s dismissal of its former AI leader. Desiring more and frustrated by what he sees as a reluctance to embrace AI with the same urgency as competitors, Ternus pushed his software chief to accelerate the company’s road map and move quickly beyond merely making necessary improvements to Siri.

A large part of Federighi’s approach has been how hands-on he is, resulting in his viewpoint shaping how Apple’s software development goes to a large extent. That’s left team members feeling that they have ‘limited influence’ over what happens:

Federighi is known for taking an especially hands-on role in meetings and major decisions, with some employees describing a management style that can leave little room for competing ideas to gain traction. He is also known for vigorously debating opposing viewpoints, using his command of technical details and ability to argue his position to push back against proposals he disagrees with. That approach has at times frustrated members of the software engineering and design teams, some of whom believe they have limited influence over the direction of products.

Another part is that Federighi reportedly only plans for a 12-18 month timeframe, versus the years-long roadmaps used in hardware engineering. These are some of the gaps that Apple may want to bridge in the coming years.

Separately, Vision Pro and Siri AI leader Mike Rockwell is still seriously considering stepping down. He’s currently vice president of Siri engineering:

The management concerns go beyond Federighi. Mike Rockwell, who created the Vision Pro headset in addition to leading the development of Siri AI, continues to seriously consider stepping down. That could mean leaving his role as vice president of Siri engineering — or Apple altogether — next year. Such a move would be a major blow, given his role in bringing the new Siri to market after an embarrassing two-year delay.

Ternus should put back the fear in the software/UX design teams' eyes again like Jobs did. Ternus can start with firing Craig Federighi to show that no one is safe.

Then maybe they would have a lot less bugs, bad UX decisions and add that polish Apple software used to have.

In the same report, Gurman also reported that Apple is experimenting with a new development pipeline, giving engineers 50% more time to code and explore more, and spend less time on integration, as coding agents have improved.

It remains to be seen what else might change at Apple to enable faster development timelines, though it seems likely that Craig Federighi may have to release his ‘firm grip’ on the software engineering org, and instead allow some other leaders to blossom and act on their own accord.

Official | Waldemar Anton withdraws from Germany squad, Ridle Baku earns late call-up

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Germany head coach Jürgen Klopp has recalled RB Leipzig’s Ridle Baku to the national team after Borussia Dortmund defender Waldemar Anton withdrew from the upcoming UEFA Nations League fixtures against Serbia and the Netherlands. Dortmund confirmed on Sunday afternoon that the 30-year-old centre-back would be unavailable for international duty because of muscular problems. The German Football Association subsequently announced that Baku would replace Anton in Klopp’s squad.

The development comes just three days after Klopp announced his original 24-man squad for November’s international fixtures. Baku was among several prominent players omitted from the original selection, despite featuring in Klopp’s first Germany squad during the September and October international window. Baku did not end up logging any minutes in Klopp’s first four fixtures in charge of die Nationalmannschaft.

The 28-year-old Leipzig defender has recently demonstrated his attacking qualities at club level, scoring in Wednesday’s 3-2 Champions League defeat against Manchester City. He also started Saturday’s 5-2 Bundesliga victory over Augsburg. Overall, Baku has scored three goals and contributed one assist in 13 appearances for Leipzig across all competitions this season.

Baku’s Leipzig teammate Brajan Gruda will also join Klopp’s squad following an outstanding individual performance in Saturday’s 5-2 victory over Augsburg. The 22-year-old attacking midfielder recorded two assists inside the space of two minutes, setting up Ezechiel Banzuzi’s 34th-minute equalizer before providing an excellent chipped pass for Christopher Nkunku to put Leipzig ahead.

Gruda ultimately finished with five chances created, ten successful dribbles from eleven attempts, and eleven successful ground duels from thirteen contests. He was subsequently voted the Bundesliga’s Player of the Match.

Both head coaches praised the youngster afterward.

“Of course, everyone saw what kind of player Brajan is and what a performance he delivered today,” Leipzig trainer Martin Demichelis remarked at his post-match press conference. “That makes me happy.”

“I’d point to Gruda,” Manuel Baum added in his own opening statement at the press conference.  “He was really difficult to defend against and found solutions in numerous situations.”

While Baku has earned a late recall and Gruda prepares for another opportunity with Germany, Leipzig captain David Raum remains outside Klopp’s squad. The 28-year-old left-back was omitted from the original selection on Thursday, with Klopp preferring Bayern Munich’s Nathaniel Brown and Stuttgart’s Maximilian Mittelstädt. The latter scored the winning goal for his Bundesliga outfit in Stuttgart’s 1-0 Saturday evening win over Werder Bremen.

Raum responded with arguably his strongest performance of the season against Augsburg, scoring his first goal of the campaign with a superb direct free kick shortly before half-time. Speaking to DAZN afterward, Raum expressed frustration with recent criticism surrounding his performances and international prospects.

“Everything that’s been reported about me and all the surrounding noise over the past few weeks has, to put it bluntly, pissed me off,” Raum explained. “Today was the time to provide an answer, and that’s what I did with my free-kick goal.”

The Leipzig captain nevertheless made clear that he respected Klopp’s decision.

“Of course it’s disappointing. I’d be lying if I said otherwise,” Raum told Sky Germany. “I also told him quite clearly that I’d respond with performances so that he won’t be able to overlook me next time. I took the first step toward that today.”

Vitalik Buterin says ETH should be valued as a store of value, not a fee-burning token

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The Ethereum co-founder said the case for valuing ETH as a fee-burning token "never was as strong as it seemed" and that maximizing fees would force Ethereum to operate like a major corporation.

Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin said he believes ether Ethereum (ETH) should be treated primarily as a store of value, arguing that the case for valuing it as a fee-burning token was weaker than it appeared during the last market cycle.

In an X thread on Sunday morning, Buterin said "ETH as store of value" makes "much more sense to focus on" than "ETH as fee-burning token." He said EIP-1559, the 2021 upgrade that burns part of every transaction fee, "is fine and good," and the network should keep the mechanism and its burn, even if the burn only partially offsets the issuance of new ETH. "But we should not feel sad if fee burn is < 10% of breakeven indefinitely; the value of ETH comes from elsewhere," he wrote.

Buterin argued instead that the main thing users appreciate about Ethereum is its decentralization and efforts toward censorship resistance, privacy, and security, and that the network's main practical benefit to the financial ecosystem is its stability. "In the era of AI disruption, stability will be highly valued," he wrote.

The fee burn was the basis of the "ultrasound money" thesis that gained traction among ETH holders during the 2021 bull market. Buterin, in his thread, laid out the valuation model behind fee-burning tokens, in which a token's market cap would equal the annual burn divided by a discount rate. Under that model, a token burning $1 billion a year at a 4% discount rate would have a natural valuation floor of $25 billion, he wrote.

"But here's the problem: this was never a true floor," Buterin wrote. He said most of the ETH burned in 2021 through 2023 came from "crypto-internal use cases" whose revenue would have fallen alongside ETH's price, citing the network "randomly [going] crazy" for a "digital monkey sale" that burned $180 million in fees in a single day. "The floor argument only works if the backing of the revenue stream is external," he wrote.

(Actually, the sale Buterin referenced was for digital land in the Otherside metaverse from Yuga Labs, the creators of the Bored Ape Yacht Club NFT collection. The sale saw users paying up to $10,000 in transaction fees for Otherdeed NFTs that currently sell for around $150.)

Buterin said Ethereum now has a "better claim to this external backing" because of the growing market for tokenized real-world assets. But as a "normal stable mature business," that activity is unlikely to produce the same fee spikes, he added.

The amount of ETH burned each day has fallen sharply since then, according to The Block's data. Ethereum burned about 1.48 million ETH in 2022, worth roughly $3.6 billion, compared with about 19,200 ETH, or $43 million, so far this year.

Buterin said that justifying ETH's market cap, which he put at about $270 billion, through fee burn alone would make Ethereum comparable to a company like L'Oreal. Ether's market cap stood at $273.3 billion on Sunday morning, according to The Block's ETH price page.

Companies of that size spend heavily to keep their positions, Buterin wrote. Citing an AI estimate that L’Oreal spends about 0.58% of its market cap on research and development each year, he said matching that rate would “bankrupt the [Ethereum Foundation] almost immediately,” so it would instead require in-protocol funding of about 700,000 ETH a year, cutting validator revenue by two-thirds.

What Buterin left unstated: the level of funding received by the Ethereum Foundation (EF) is already somewhat controversial in the crypto community.

The EF's ETH sales have regularly drawn criticism from holders, including after it sold 20,000 ETH worth about $47 million to Bitmine over two weeks in April and May. Buterin said in May that he regularly hears from community members who feel the EF's actions don't match its public commitments.

The EF began staking part of its treasury earlier this year to generate yield, and in March it published a mandate describing itself as "one of many stewards" of the network. Amid a wave of senior staff departures in May, Buterin said the foundation would become "a smaller ship" that sells less ETH and focuses on work that "would not happen otherwise."

Buterin also said that an Ethereum optimizing for token burn would need "highly interventionist governance, for the long term," and that neither requirement is compatible with his goals for the network of capture resistance and decentralization. Buterin added that optimizing for fee burn means optimizing for higher gas prices to some degree, which "may price out users, including some users who may need privacy and security the most."

Buterin said that when he asks people what they value most about Ethereum, they most often cite decentralization and what he calls CROPS (censorship and capture resistance, open source, privacy and security). For an investor holding ETH as a store of value (SoV), "the first and most important SoV-ish metric" is "to survive," he wrote.

He tied that goal to themes from the most recent of the network's developer conferences, including post-quantum security, partial ossification of the base layer and a high bar for adding new precompiles or in-protocol rewards. Buterin listed work on multisig wallets, the Kohaku privacy project, the FOCIL censorship-resistance proposal and lightweight nodes as examples, noting that he runs a node on his laptop that synced in under six hours and takes up "only 1/8 of my hard drive."

Buterin said growth in assets issued natively on Ethereum's base layer, including real-world assets, is "highly synergistic" with the store-of-value case because it expands what holders can do with ETH, such as using it as collateral.

Buterin views Ethereum as "really not just a blockchain anymore," but rather "a hybrid architecture that combines together blockchains and modern cryptography." In September, he mapped out Ethereum's roadmap through 2030 and said the upcoming Hegota upgrade is likely to be the network's last "normal" fork.

Buterin's post garnered support from other influential figures in crypto. Solana co-founder Anatoly Yakovenko said Buterin "is absolutely right that you can't focus on fees as the product." Attorney Gabriel Shapiro of MetaLeX called the thread "a 'nature is healing' moment," while Moonrock Capital founder Simon Dedic argued that ETH as a store of value would be "the biggest bull case for Ethereum. And at the same time the easiest story to sell to institutional capital."

The thread comes about five weeks after the co-authors of controversial proposal EIP-8363 withdrew it from consideration for the network's forthcoming Hegota upgrade, which will follow the behind-schedule Glamsterdam hard fork. The proposal, which would have burned a rising share of validator rewards as more ETH is staked, cited the risk a high staking ratio poses to "ETH's role as money" and drew opposition from SharpLink CEO Joseph Chalom and Aave founder Stani Kulechov. Its authors plan to hold a separate issuance process that runs through EthCC in April.

ETH traded at $2,236 as of 9:23 a.m. ET, down 0.3% on the day, according to The Block's price page.

Sponsored content and press releases are labeled as such and are not written by our editorial team.

AI cyber resilience and the trust gap

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

BREAKING ANALYSIS by Dave Vellante and Krista Case

Backup and recovery has evolved into data protection, security and cyber recovery. Today, the focus is on business resilience. The scope continues to expand, with growing attention to how attackers use artificial intelligence and how defenders can use it to better protect their assets.

In new field research with our partner Qualitate, we explored these issues, including a less frequently discussed risk: What happens when AI deployed inside an enterprise makes a mistake?

Our research indicates that confidence in recovery is running well ahead of capabilities. The study underscores the immaturity of cyber resilience and AI security. Agentic AI is putting more pressure on practitioners to secure their data, reconstruct an agent’s actions, understand what it was trying to accomplish and confidently restore the business to a trusted state. The risks created by an enterprise’s own AI deployments are becoming a growing part of the resilience challenge.

Welcome to Breaking Analysis No. 333, AI cyber resilience and the trust gap. In this episode, we welcome back Krista Case, who recently conducted in-depth interviews with 25 security and cyber resilience practitioners as part of theCUBE Research’s Cybersecurity & Resilience Summit.

Access the full slide deck and appendix with survey details and practitioner quotes.

Information technology and security practitioners face two growing pressures: 1) Protecting the business from adversaries using AI and 2) Putting guardrails in place as their own organizations move AI projects into production. Our study examined how organizations are approaching security and resilience across both fronts, with particular attention paid to the risks created by their own AI deployments.

We explored how organizations are managing agent identity, permissions, monitoring, etc., alongside their preparations for incident response and recovery. A central question was what happens when an agent takes an incorrect action using legitimate permissions. That scenario raises three questions:

These questions shaped our study into how organizations are preparing for AI to take a more active role in their operations. As agents gain the authority to act, practitioners need to understand how a mistake could spread and what recovery would require.

Ultimately, the study examined organizations’ readiness to secure their own AI deployments and recover from mistakes made by authorized agents, alongside the continuing need to defend against adversarial AI.

Our study identified just four of 25 organizations as having exercised an AI or agent incident scenario, including partial exercises. We believe this finding exposes a core trust gap at the center of the research: Confidence in recovery often runs ahead of what organizations have actually tested.

These findings come from a small sample of in-depth interviews conducted with our partner Qualitate. The conversations allowed us to explore what customers have tested, the additional security work their own AI creates and what it takes to resume a business process safely. One healthcare practitioner phrased the disconnect between confidence and readiness as follows:

No, we have not completed any tabletop exercises as it pertains to an AI agent or other similar attacks. We’re highly confident because these are scenarios similar to non-AI incidents we have handled before.

— Senior manager, information security operations, healthcare and biotech

Across the interviews, respondents who did express confidence often drew heavily on existing ransomware and incident response playbooks. Their experience with non-AI incidents understandably provides a degree of trust. But it also raises a concern in that organizations may assume that an agent taking an incorrect action can be handled in the same way as an insider or external attacker doing something similar. We don’t believe existing playbooks cover all scenarios.

Rather, we believe that assumption needs to be tested against the specific demands of an agent incident. The interviews revealed a mixed picture of true validation:

Complete business recovery requires an organization to reconstruct a sequence of authorized actions, identify incorrect outcomes, repair downstream effects and safely resume the affected business process. Demonstrating that the full sequence works is a nontrivial exercise.

The speed, scale and interconnected nature of agent activity makes that even more challenging. Actions can affect multiple systems and processes, creating consequences that existing playbooks need to account for. We see a risk of false confidence when organizations assume that their experience with traditional non-AI incidents will translate automatically. Testing needs to establish how well their capabilities hold up under the conditions an agent can create, which may be novel.

Bottom line: Evidence of validated, end-to-end business recovery from an agent incident remains limited in our study. We believe organizations should exercise their existing playbooks against realistic agent scenarios, with safe business resumption as the measure of success.

Our interviews raised questions about tooling, maturity and the processes needed to prove that existing capabilities work for agentic AI. Two findings as shown below highlight the limited reach of detection and reconstruction capabilities in the organizations studied:

One healthcare practitioner’s assessment of the controls already implemented underscores the trust gap:

No, for the ones that have been implemented, we have not tested or validated that they work.

— Senior manager, information security operations, healthcare and biotech

Enterprises already have identity systems, application logs, data protection tools and the like, along with established incident response processes. They are trying to extend these capabilities to AI. The central challenge is validating that the tools can reconstruct activity across an entire agent workflow.

Agents interact with multiple applications, invoke tools and autonomously trigger downstream actions. Each system may capture its own events without providing a coherent record of the complete workflow. Incident response requires correlating those records to establish which agent acted and under what authority, then tracing the tools it invoked and the changes that resulted.

This reminds us of the early days of disaster recovery, when organizations would test failover but hesitate to test failback because they were nervous about what might happen. We see a similar reluctance to ask questions that could expose gaps in readiness or cause downstream problems. The concern is whether the connected tools and processes will hold up under realistic agent incident conditions.

Bottom line: Organizations need to exercise the full agent workflow under realistic conditions, including a plausible worst-case event, and validate that their tools and processes can reconstruct the activity and support the response.

Our assessment of the field interviews highlights both the technical and business dimensions involved in a recovery. An AI agent can use legitimate access to change a record incorrectly or send a message that should never have been sent. A backup may work and the restored systems may appear healthy while the business remains unable to resume safely.

StorageTek veteran Fred Moore, an early mentor to Dave Vellante, coined the phrase: “Backup is one thing, recovery is everything.” The scope of recovery continues to expand as organizations consider the consequences of agents acting across business systems.

A director of information security at a media and entertainment firm described the problem this way:

We could restore our systems, we could restore within our recovery objectives, but that doesn’t necessarily guarantee that it’s safe. The bottleneck here would be trust.

— Director of information security, media and entertainment

Recovery point objectives or RPO and recovery time objectives or RTO are the so-called business metrics. But business users need more meaningful key performance indicators that resonate — specifically, what a trusted, viable service looks like and how that condition can be verified. That includes establishing who needs to participate in recovery and who signs off that operations can safely resume.

This direction needs to come from the top down; and be verified bottom up. Business leaders must define the requirements, with IT and security acting as partners to provide the underlying technology and help achieve the required outcome. The responsibility extends beyond the traditional data protection team’s work of recovering data and systems.

Incorrect transactions, human error and failures that ripple across systems have existed for a long time. Agentic AI adds two complications:

That requires a comprehensive view of what went wrong and what needs to happen to recover. The organization must be able to trace the agent’s activity and evaluate the resulting business state against clear criteria for safe operation.

Bottom line: Recovery requires explicit criteria for verifying that a business service can be trusted and clear responsibility for approving its return to operation. The business must set that direction, with IT and security providing the capabilities to carry it out.

Humans in the loop help organizations restrict what agents can do. Resilience also has to account for what happens when a human misses an incorrect action or cannot keep pace with agents operating at machine speed. Two statements from the same CIO illustrates the issue:

So the given scenario will be detected by human beings before the AI agents can take the actions.

If it really happens, currently we do not have the capability to roll back the agent’s action.

— Chief information officer, real estate and construction

The first statement shows confidence that human oversight will catch the problem. The second acknowledges a missing “undo command” if the human oversight fails. This captures why approvals and recovery testing need to advance together. Reliance on human intervention can bring a false sense of security and defer attention to the recovery details needed when a mistake gets through.

Our field study also revealed how customers are weighing the tradeoffs around autonomy, with organizations generally still containing what their agents can do. Those decisions become particularly important when agents receive authority to take remedial actions during an incident or recovery.

The level of autonomy granted should reflect the risk of the action. Before allowing an agent to approve or execute a remedial action, organizations need to understand the consequences of an incorrect decision. That calls for a tiered approach built around two considerations:

We expect human approval to become an increasingly significant bottleneck over the next six to 12 months. Organizations need to establish these boundaries as they begin trusting agents to take more remedial actions.

Bottom line: Human oversight and recovery testing need to develop together. As organizations delegate more authority to agents, they must account for mistakes, the limits of human review and the risk associated with each action.

A retail practitioner described a viable approach to rehearsing an agent incident and we see this as a best practice. Specifically, this firm replays the agent’s actions in a replica environment, investigating the root cause and using granular snapshots to understand the transactional effects. The respondent explicitly confirmed that the approach had been tested in the sandbox with the following statement:

Yes, if the agent went across multiple systems, my approach is to replay this within my sandbox environment, which is a full replica of all of the systems.

— Director of information technology, retail

The value of this approach is in reproducing the sequence of events and investigating their effects across systems. Our assessment of what such an exercise should confirm is anchored on three outcomes:

We see these as minimum viable expectations for the evidence an exercise should produce.

We use the term digital twin here as a broad analogy for this approach. A fuller digital twin would involve a richer representation of business processes, dependencies and state. We believe the industry still has work to do in that direction, and that work will become more important as enterprises use AI to run critical functions. In addition, it will further expand the notion of recovery as the state of the business will become increasingly more complicated as agents perform more of the work.

The respondent also acknowledged a limitation saying: larger transaction volumes could require more time for reconciliation. Replaying actions and identifying affected transactions provides useful information, while the time needed to resolve their consequences remains an open item. Richer simulations of business environments could help organizations test agent behavior and prepare for what a failure might look like in production.

In other words, testing the recovery sequence in a variety of scenarios inside a digital twin of the business prior to pushing the process into production. Further, this “red teaming” exercise must be ongoing – expanding the concept of continuous data protection into the state of a firm’s operations in more granular snapshots.

Bottom line: This example offers a practical approach to reproducing agent actions, investigating their consequences and rehearsing recovery. Its value also points toward the need for richer representations of the business processes those agents affect.

If a chief information security officer takes one action from this study, we recommend exercising an agent incident affecting one consequential workflow already in production. Choose a workflow in which an agent has permission to act across multiple systems, and design the exercise to include incorrect actions taken with valid credentials and permissions.

The exercise should also include a scenario requiring human approval. This helps establish the boundaries around where autonomy is appropriate and where human oversight remains necessary.

Validation by the business owner is essential. Security teams can establish that an incident has been contained and provide evidence of what happened. The business owner must determine whether the resulting business state is acceptable. That decision needs to account for the consequences of the agent’s actions and any remediation work still required.

The exercise should document what was proved, what remains untested and which teams are responsible for closing the remaining gaps. That evidence should also inform decisions about how much authority an agent receives. The organization needs to understand its ability to reverse or correct actions and recover their consequences as it considers granting greater autonomy.

The discussions at the AI Cybersecurity and Resilience Summit reinforced the themes emerging from the field study. Conversations with vendors, practitioners and researchers underscored the need to build resilience and recovery into agent deployments from the outset. Evidence that these capabilities work becomes essential as agentic workloads move into production.

The challenge includes preserving and recovering a trusted business state when an agent produces an incorrect outcome despite having legitimate credentials. Organizations need to understand how those outcomes affect their operations and what it will take to address them.

Existing investments in data protection infrastructure, security and recovery remain critical. The work ahead is to identify the gaps that emerge under the new requirements and pressures created by enterprise AI. That requires collaboration across internal teams and with vendors to understand where capabilities need to improve.

Bottom line: We believe trust in enterprise AI requires tested evidence that organizations can understand what agents have done, stop them, reconstruct their actions and recover to a healthy business state. Our research indicates that organizations still have work to do to demonstrate that their existing security and recovery capabilities can meet that requirement.

Tencent AI chief warns models will soon pose biology risks, SCMP reports

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Yao Shunyu spoke in Hong Kong days after Tencent launched Hy4, which it trained without offensive hacking data.

Tencent’s chief AI scientist has warned that AI models will soon be able to cause harm in biology, as they already can in cybersecurity. Yao Shunyu spoke at a science event in Hong Kong on Saturday, the South China Morning Post reported.

“The cyber abilities of [AI] models and soon [the] bio ability of models will reach a threshold where it’s actually possible to do harm,” Yao said, according to the SCMP.

He said Chinese companies that release open models, which anyone can download and change, need to take safety very seriously. He also warned that biology risks may be harder to manage than cyber risks.

Yao, 29, joined Tencent last year after a year at OpenAI. He spoke days after Tencent launched Hy4, its new flagship model, on 4 November.

In its launch announcement, Tencent said it left material on exploiting software flaws out of Hy4’s training data. The model was instead trained on defensive security data, the company said.

Tencent also said that Hy4 has strong basic knowledge of biology, but is weaker than leading models at designing experiments. It said it based this on internal expert reviews. TNW has not tested the model.

The company plans to release Hy4’s weights and a report on its safety testing in the coming days, Reuters reported. Hy4 ranks as the second-best Chinese model, according to benchmarking firm Artificial Analysis, the SCMP said.

Yao said US firms such as Anthropic make a strong case for keeping top models closed. However, he argued that a mix of open and closed models may work best, because open models can also help defenders.

Princess Kate Pairs Her Polka-Dot Midi Dress With a Meaningful Pair of Family Jewels

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

She and Prince William attended the Festival of Remembrance event in London

Every year, the Festival of Remembrance welcomes people from all over the country to Royal Albert Hall for an evening of celebration, honoring active members of the military, veterans, and all the families who have lost loved ones in the armed forces. With musical performances and personal testimonies, the Saturday evening event kicks off Remembrance Sunday, the official day of remembrance in the United Kingdom.

As always, members of the royal family made an appearance at the annual festival. But this year saw a particularly strong turnout, with King Charles III and Queen Camilla in attendance, as well as Prince William and Princess Kate.

For the occasion, Kate, always intentional with her style choices, put together a look full of meaningful pieces fitting for such a meaningful event. Her black-and-white polka-dot dress, for starters, was the same piece worn to Prince Philip’s memorial service back in 2022. Courtesy of Alessandra Rich, it featured a high mock neckline and long sleeves, with a drop-waist pleated midi skirt and a black belt accent. The princess updated the piece by adding a red poppy—the symbol of remembrance—and her silver Irish Guards regimental brooch, which represented her role as Colonel of the Irish Guards.

For jewelry, the future queen brought a piece of her late grandmother-in-law with her, wearing a sparkling pair of diamond earrings that once belonged to Queen Elizabeth II. The statement jewels featured a cluster of diamonds that created a floral-shaped stud, from which an additional strand of diamonds hung, framed by an outer oval frame of... yes, more diamonds.

Adventure Links of the Week

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

When we’re not outdoors, we get our adventure fix by exploring social media and the web. Here are some of the best adventure links we’ve discovered this week.

Yosemite Visitors Join Forces To Perform Extraordinary Rescue on Half Dome: When a Polish hiker slipped while descending the cables on Yosemite’s Half Dome, she slid down the steep granite face and was left clinging to the rock. Her fiancé then lost his footing while trying to reach her.

A group of strangers came together to rescue the pair. Using ropes, slings, carabiners, and climbing harnesses, the group formed a human chain to reach both stranded hikers and pull them to safety.

The Electric Arms Race Has Gone Too Far: Tim Shuff questions the growing use of electric motors in outdoor adventure. He argues that the speed and convenience they create undermine traditionally self-propelled activities like paddling and cycling. Today, you can even buy battery-powered SUPs.

While recognizing that electric assistance can make outdoor sports more accessible, he thinks that its wider use will change wilderness areas, reshape trails, and create pressure to build increasingly powerful equipment.

Jan Spicka descends from Huascarán Sur. Photo: Matej Zak

A Czech Alpinist Describes How He Survived a Nightmare on Peru’s Highest Mountain: Climbing Peru’s Huascarán Sur turned into a tragedy for a group of five friends last July. Czech alpinist Jan Spicka describes how he and partner Matej Zak reached the 6,768m summit before he plunged into a hidden crevasse on the descent.

Matej’s quick reactions saved his life, but their three friends, Saul Mendoza, Chicho Ugarte, and Arti Salas, disappeared while attempting to descend by a different route. With no official mountain rescue in Peru, friends and volunteers pitched in. After 24 hours in the freezing conditions, they knew it was unlikely they would see their friends again. GPS tracks and later searches suggest they had fallen through a collapsing snow bridge.

He Felt Stuck in New York. So He Started Walking to California: Feeling trapped by unemployment and a lack of opportunities in New York, 29-year-old Chantz Rivera began walking across America. He set off with a tent, sleeping bag, and some supplies in a shopping cart. Over 149 days, he walked to California, pushing his cart 32 to 40km a day along highways and through small towns, documenting his progress for his growing number of online followers.

This Mountain Race Makes Runners Carry 60kg of Beer and Supplies Uphill: Slovakia’s annual Sherpa Rallye is one of the world’s unique mountain races. Competitors have to haul heavy loads of supplies up a 5.7km rocky trail to a remote hut in the High Tatras, nearly 2,000m above sea level.

Men carry 60kg, including a 30-liter keg of beer, while women carry 20kg, with the loads secured to wooden frames. The race was started in 1985 by mountain porter Viktor Beranek. The event brings together the region’s nosiči (traditional mountain porters) and also helps stock the remote hut ahead of winter.

A Hiker Slid Hundreds of Meters Down Mount Timpanogos: A female hiker was seriously injured after slipping off the trail near the summit of Mount Timpanogos in Utah. She slid several hundred feet down the steep mountainside before landing on a narrow cliff ledge. Newly released helicopter footage shows her being hoisted to safety. She was taken to hospital and is reportedly recovering.

We Need to Move Past the ‘Shut Up and Die Like a Man’ Mentality in the Mountains: Climber and paraglider Will Gadd reflects on the psychological impact of mountain accidents. Climbing culture often glorifies the dangers people face in the mountains while discouraging them from admitting they are struggling.

Gadd discusses the deaths of two close friends and challenges the underlying expectation that climbers should simply suck it up and carry on. After decades in the mountains, he has seen that the consequences of an accident extend far beyond the immediate incident, affecting survivors and their families and friends for years.

Two Hikers Dead in Bear Attack in British Columbia: Two hikers, identified as 48-year-old Jennifer Balcarras and her 20-year-old daughter Selah Reed, have died in a suspected grizzly attack near the Snooka West Trail in Bella Coola, British Columbia. Their bodies were found during a search after the pair were reported missing by their family.

This comes just a year after a separate grizzly attack in the same area severely injured four people, including three children. The trail remains closed as authorities continue their investigation.

Rebecca McPhee is a freelance writer for ExplorersWeb.

Rebecca has been writing about open water sports, adventure travel, and marine science for three years. Prior to that, Rebecca worked as an Editorial Assistant at Taylor and Francis, and a Wildlife Officer for ORCA.

Based in the UK Rebecca is a science teacher and volunteers for a number of marine charities. She enjoys open water swimming, hiking, diving, and traveling.

Apple aiming to make Siri ‘more like an agent’ as soon as next year: report

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Apple finally shipped its long awaited personalized Siri with iOS 27. The company lived up to its promises, and delivered on its hefty goals that it set out back in 2024.

That said, Siri still lacks in terms of proactively taking actions for you, a feature that many AI companies are actively working towards. Apple might come around on that next year, per Bloomberg’s Mark Gurman. The company is also experimenting with a new engineering pipeline that might make faster development possible.

In recent months, companies like Meta and SpaceXSI have gone all-in on new agentic AI interfaces that can carry out tasks for you. Muse and Grok Bot are agents that have their own virtual machines and can proactively handle tasks in the background for you, then deliver results.

Apple has a lot of potential to deliver in this space, especially given all of the frameworks they’ve built up over the years, like App Intents. With Apple, you hypothetically wouldn’t need to depend on as much computer-use ability, making certain tasks a lot less expensive.

According to Mark Gurman in his Power On newsletter, Apple could debut its own proactive version of Siri as soon as next year:

Dots, Muse and Grok can keep track of goals, determine what needs to happen next and continue pursuing objectives without the user initiating every individual step. Apple ultimately wants to go in this direction, I’m told, and I believe the company could make Siri more like an agent as early as next year — not that it has much of a choice, given current consumer and market demands.

It remains to be seen what Apple will ultimately ship here, but with the right execution, Apple certainly has high potential to compete in this space.

An agentic Siri would be a welcome addition. I’d love to see Apple focus on deploying this for tasks like scheduling appointments or making informational calls—things humans don't necessarily need to do themselves. Of course, it would need to be highly usable and built with privacy in mind. If implemented well, it could be a fantastic use of AI

A major part of better execution is having a better process. According to Gurman, Apple is also giving engineers more time to code and explore, rather than spend as much time integrating features within the operating system.

Typically, each of these stages is four weeks. With the first cycle of iOS 28 development, Apple is allotting 6 weeks to coding and 2 weeks to integration. This change was likely driven by the massive advances in agentic coding tools in the past couple years:

For this cycle, though, Apple has done things differently for the M1 milestone. Instead of the normal split between two four-week efforts, Apple engineers were tasked with six weeks of coding, exploration and feature development, and then only two weeks of convergence. Employees say they like the change, indicating that it creates more time for coming up with ideas and exploring them before needing to lock things in.

This Glass Barn Redefines Modern Country Living—and It’s Fully Furnished

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

An eye-catching glass barn with a 28-foot great room in New Hope, PA, is now being offered fully furnished for $5.5 million.

Built in 1987 and originally designed by Richard Olcott and Todd Schliemann of Polshek & Partners, the four-bedroom Bucks County home with an attached two-car garage was entirely reimagined in 2020.

“From the inside and out, the wall of glass and enormous foyer are a remarkable statement,” says listing agent Ali Jankowski of Kurfiss Sotheby’s International Realty. “It’s a special and spectacular home in all ways.”

The 6,670-square-foot, industrial-style home features concrete walls, contemporary works of art, and multiple fireplaces.

The chef's kitchen has soapstone countertops, diamond-plate steel cabinetry, professional-grade appliances, and a cozy seating area with a wood-burning fireplace.

“The kitchen is super functional and in vibe with the rest of the house,” Jankowski notes. “The gathering spaces on the property are just incredible.”

The great room has a floor-to-ceiling, wood-burning concrete fireplace and polished-concrete heated floors with reclaimed hardwood accents.

“The next buyer will be someone who is creative and has an appreciation for architecture,” she says. “They won’t be looking for something traditional but will want something very unique. The spaces are open and bright and designed in relation to galleries.”

Other highlights include a 1,200-bottle, glass-walled wine room and a primary suite with a circular, glass-walled sitting room with a suspended fireplace, a spa-inspired bath with a skylit stone shower, and a boutique-style dressing room.

There's also a circular-walled library, a studio, a home office, and a sun-soaked lower-level recreation room.

“The sellers live in the home year-round, but it’s low maintenance, so it could easily be used as a secondary residence,” Jankowski says. “The next buyer might be someone who has cool art they want to display or are interested in living in art. It’s truly its own piece of art.”

The professionally landscaped 6.32-acre lot comes with a black-bottom heated pool with a sun shelf and spa, as well as sports courts.

“The pool area is removed from the home, so you’re not looking at it in the winter when it’s closed,” she says. “It’s kind of like its own destination.”

A glass greenhouse designed for entertaining has dual wood-burning fireplaces and seating for up to 20 guests.

“The greenhouse is amazing,” she says. “Many a dinner party has been held there.”

Jankowski adds tht the home should be relatively easy to maintain as a second residence.

“A lot of homes in Bucks County are old, so you'd better have a property manager,” she notes. However, “this home was fully redone with new systems and a new roof in 2020, so there’s not much to worry about.”

New Hope is also easily accessible from New York City and Philadelphia.

“There are rolling hills, beautiful topography, and it’s a river town,” she says. “What’s not to love?”

Joshua Kimmich dedicates first goal of 2026/27 Bundesliga campaign to late father

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Bayern Munich midfielder Joshua Kimmich has spoken movingly about the recent death of his father after dedicating his goal in Saturday’s 2-1 Bundesliga victory over Mainz 05 to his memory. The 31-year-old German international celebrated his 14th-minute opener by leaping into the air before pointing toward the heavens.

Kimmich’s father Berthold passed away in October at the age of 57. The midfielder subsequently missed three competitive fixtures before returning to action in last weekend’s 2-2 draw against Borussia Dortmund.Speaking after Saturday’s victory, Kimmich acknowledged that returning to the football pitch had brought its own emotional challenges.

“Because of what happened with my dad, it was very emotional for me,” Kimmich told Kicker. “I actually thought about him immediately. Normally, you block those thoughts out on the football pitch. At the moment, however, being on the pitch is actually the most difficult part. When I’m playing football, that’s when I think about him most often, because football connected us so deeply. That’s why today was very emotional.”

“I don’t score that many goals anyway,” Kimmich continued. “Naturally, I thought about him immediately after scoring. That’s why it was definitely more emotional than usual.”

Kimmich’s personal bereavement comes amid another challenging situation for Bayern, with head coach Vincent Kompany currently unavailable due to illness. The midfielder nevertheless emphasized the strength of the relationships within the squad and coaching staff, suggesting that the club’s current unity has helped everyone navigate the difficulties.

“What we’ve built together over the past few years is very special,” Kimmich explained. “The players, the coaching staff, and everyone around us have a great relationship with one another and support each other in every situation.

“With our coach being unavailable, that’s become clear once again. I’ve experienced things differently during my eleven years at Bayern.”

Teammate Aleksandar Pavlovic echoed the sentiment.

“We live the ‘Mia san Mia’ mentality and are a family both on and off the pitch,” Pavlovic said. “Especially over the past few weeks, we’ve been playing for our coach and the players who aren’t available. We’ve been doing that very well recently.”

Kimmich also acknowledged that Saturday’s victory was hardly Bayern’s most convincing display. Nevertheless, the midfielder felt the team had defended its lead professionally and avoided allowing Mainz a meaningful opportunity to overturn the result.

For Kimmich personally, however, the moment that will endure from the afternoon in Mainz is likely to be the goal celebration honoring his father.

On this day in space! Nov. 8, 2005: Europe launches Venus Express, its first mission to Venus

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Venus Express had to survive harsh radiation at Venus, but it persevered for nine years.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

On Nov. 8, 2005, Europe launched its first mission to Venus. The spacecraft was called Venus Express.

Venus is a hothouse planet, one where the atmospheric pressure can easily crush an unprotected spacecraft. Even from orbit, Venus Express had to survive harsh radiation. But the spacecraft lasted for more than nine years.

During that time, Venus Express saw a weird vortex swirling around Venus's south pole. It also saw possible signs of active volcanoes. Venus Express also discovered that the planet generates its own auroras. These auroras happen when particles from the sun hit charged particles in the atmosphere.

Late in the Venus Express mission, controllers told the spacecraft to dip down into Venus's clouds — even though the spacecraft wasn't designed to do this. It performed well, but eventually it stopped listening to commands. The spacecraft burned up in the atmosphere in January 2015.

Venus Express established ESA as the third world organization to successfully send a probe to study Venus, following the United States and Soviet Union. (Japan's JAXA became the fourth five years later.)

"By studying Venus, scientists learn how Earth-like planets change over time," reads a summary of the planet's exploration prepared by The Planetary Society. "Orbital studies [like Venus Express] hint that Venus may have hosted habitable oceans before becoming inhospitable."

"By studying Venus, we can learn how Earth-like planets evolve and what shapes planets throughout the galaxy into temperate, lush worlds instead of sweltering hothouses. Venus also helps scientists model Earth’s climate, and may serve as a cautionary tale on how dramatically a planet’s climate can change," wrote the Society.

ESA returned to Venus in 2018 when BepiColombo, its joint mission with JAXA, used the planet's gravity to redirect its path toward the planet Mercury.

Want more space history? Check out our full On This Day In Space Story archive and watch our On This Day In Space videos on YouTube.

Diamond Foundry creates world's first 200mm single-crystal diamond wafer

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

It has taken 25 years to get here and 300mm diamond wafers are in its sights for 2030.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Diamonds have exceptional cooling capabilities due to their excellent thermal conductivity, with the material estimated to be at least five times more conductive than copper. Because of this, Diamond Foundry, a firm focusing on lab-grown diamonds, announced the world’s first 200mm single-crystal diamond (SCD) wafer. The 200mm SCD is about the same wafer size that semiconductor fabs use for older processes, with the company boasting on its website that “Single-crystal diamond has entered the wafer-scale era.” This is part of the effort to manufacture SCD at scale so that it can be used for semiconductor cooling, especially now that increasing power of AI chips has also led to enormous thermal problems.

What makes Diamond Foundry’s 200mm SCD wafer different from other lab-grown diamonds is that it’s created as a high-quality single-crystal piece instead of a polycrystalline gem. Although the former is difficult to manufacture, it is what allows diamond to become an extreme efficient thermal conductor while simultaneously retaining its electrical insulating properties — this efficiency should help cut down on hotspots, making SCDs an excellent solution for AI chip cooling.

The company also showed in a graph that it is now thought possible to create a D300 SCD wafer before 2030, matching the size of the wafers used in leading node processes. However, it should be noted that the sample shown by the company remains unpolished, meaning it still needs to go through some processes to make it suitable for chipmakers.

Although the company was founded in 2012, it also acknowledged the work of scientists who have been chipping away at the scale problem of SCDs since the early 2000s. “This is one of those milestones that once seemed almost impossible. Hundreds of DF engineers, scientists, and technicians contributed to us getting here. It is the accumulated result of countless experiments, failures, inventions, rebuilt machines, and insights over more than a decade,” Diamond Foundry CEO Martin Roscheisen said.

“Stefan and Martin pursued this goal for 25 years. There are very few examples in technology of someone sticking to a really hard scientific challenge with that level of perseverance. They kept going when the prevailing view was that single‑crystal diamond simply could not be made at this scale. Today, the perseverance shows as the first-ever D200mm single‑crystal diamond. And best of all, it makes D300mm predictable as well.”

Diamond Foundry isn’t the only company that has identified the potential of lab-grown diamonds for semiconductor cooling. Akash Systems secured $18.2 million in CHIPS Act funding in 2024 to produce Gallium Nitride fused with synthetic diamond, which it claims will reduce temperatures by 20 degrees C. The De Beers Group, which is known for its diamond mines in Africa, is also building a copper-plated diamond composite material aimed at improving cooling efficiency for AI, high-performance computing, and GaN RF devices. Even Frore Systems, which is known for its solid-state cooler, is diving into diamond-infused watercooling cold plates for Nvidia’s next-generation AI GPUs.

Italy blames pro-Russian hackers for leaking Tajani’s personal data

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Foreign Minister Antonio Tajani said the hackers offered to pay for details about his family.

Giorgia Meloni speaks at the Italy-Japan summit in Tokyo, 16 January 2026.

Italy’s prime minister, Giorgia Meloni, has blamed pro-Russian hackers for posting the foreign minister’s personal data on the dark web. She said the sale of officials’ data was a grave concern, in a statement carried by ANSA on Saturday.

The minister, Antonio Tajani, said on X that the hackers had been spreading his data for days. He said they also wanted more facts about him and his family, and would pay for them.

He said Italy’s support for Ukraine was the reason he had been targeted.

“I will not be intimidated in any way,” Tajani wrote, in TNW’s translation.

Neither of them named a group. But the Italian paper Il Fatto Quotidiano said the data was posted by NoName057(16), a pro-Russian hacking group, according to LaPresse. TNW has not confirmed this.

NoName057(16) is best known for flooding websites with traffic to knock them offline. In May, Dutch police seized 800 servers linked to the group.

Other officials were also targeted, LaPresse reported. They include Rome’s chief prosecutor, Francesco Lo Voi, and Ivano Gabrielli, who heads Italy’s cyber police. Rome prosecutors are investigating, it added, citing Il Fatto Quotidiano.

Tajani wants hybrid attacks at the top of the agenda at a G7 meeting in Paris on Monday, Bloomberg reported, citing ANSA. The Kremlin did not respond to Bloomberg.

CoreWeave asks Texas governor-elect to let permitted data centres finish

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

CEO Michael Intrator wrote to Gina Hinojosa, who campaigned to pause new data centres in the state.

I cloud company CoreWeave has written to Texas Governor-elect Gina Hinojosa, offering to work with her team on data centre rules. The letter from chief executive Michael Intrator was sent on Saturday, Bloomberg reported.

Intrator offered to work with her transition team on finishing projects that already have permits. He also offered help setting clear, lasting standards for new ones, Bloomberg reported.

Hinojosa, a Democrat, won Tuesday’s election after promising to pause new data centres until the state has rules for them. She has said they should pay their own way and put Texans’ water and power first, ABC13 reported.

CoreWeave appears to expect that projects already approved can go ahead. Carl Holshouser, its head of government affairs, said in a LinkedIn post that Hinojosa had made clear permitted projects can be completed.

TNW could not find Hinojosa saying this herself. Her team has not said how a pause would work.

Holshouser, who helped write the letter, said families should not pay for new power lines and plants. He added that new sites should protect local water and power.

Texas has already got tougher. In August, outgoing Governor Greg Abbott ordered regulators to pause new grid links and start auditing data centres.

Wardogs Will Ban Players Who Use Low In-Game Settings On Good PCs

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

I've never been that sweaty about playing competitive shooters. I used to run around in Counter-Strike: Global Offensive with an M249 trying to get an ace, or crouch spam in PUBG to try and make friends. But I have played with people who cranked the resolution down to 800x600 to make targets appear stretched and easier to hit, or turned shadows off completely. They're in-game settings, so it's fair game, even if it's a little OTT. Wardogs, bizarrely, disagrees.

In a new post outlining its plans for Season 3, developer Bulkhead claimed that lowering in-game settings is now a bannable offense if your computer is good enough to handle higher-end settings. Basically, the team acknowledges that lower settings offer an advantage in competitive play, but only those with lower-end hardware are allowed to use them. It's one thing to go after aimbot and wallhacks, but targeting players for their in-game settings is overreaching a little.

Wardogs has an ultra-low 'potato shadows' setting that effectively removes them to improve performance.

"In Wardogs we don't allow users to set 'potato shadows' via in-game settings unless we've detected their hardware is min spec," the announcement reads. "In Season 3, we will be forcing a reset of this setting on launch to prevent users who don't need this setting using it. If you force a workaround to this (to be clear, specifically potato shadows NOT potato mode) on higher end hardware than the users it's intended for, we will consider this bannable from Season 3."

There are already a few glaring problems with this idea. For one, not everyone will read the patch notes or look at social media, and risk being blindsided by a ban simply for changing their in-game settings—not modifying game files or downloading cheats, changing the in-game settings. There's also the risk of false flags, as computer specs only tell one side of the story. Aged or damaged hardware may no longer run optimally, meaning players need lower settings despite their specs looking good on paper.

Just make sure you set the 1,000 hours of My Name Is Mayo to private, yeah?

In-game settings exist so that you can tweak a game to your preference. Risking a ban by changing them breaks a fundamental contract between players and devs: you're supposed to be able to tinker with the settings without worrying about being punished for it. Someone may reduce their graphics simply to achieve a higher framerate, rather than to gain the upper hand. Or maybe they just like how the game looks on lower settings. The reason doesn't matter. If they're in the game, they're fair use.

Potato shadows were implemented to allow those with lower-end PCs to play Wardogs, but offering an advantage to one group of players while banning another for using it has drawn flak, and the community is baffled that an in-game setting is now considered an offense. It's a little unusual, to say the least.

One of 2026’s rising stars will get a console port, but don’t expect it any time soon.

Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake devs wanted to nod to the series' notoriously convoluted timeline

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Developers behind The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time's remake supposedly wanted to nod at the series' infamously twisty, hotly debated and convoluted timeline, but series producer Eiji Aonuma thought it would be better to instead focus purely on making the Switch 2 version the best it could be.

Eiji Aonuma said as much in an interview with Game Informer, when asked if The Legend of Zelda canon was ever considered during development. "It's funny because the staff would sometimes ask me about the possibility of including nods to the timeline or references to the other game," he recalls, "and I actually, at the time, would say, 'No, let's focus on this game. Let's make this the best version of Ocarina of Time that we can.'"

The Zelda timeline notably diverges into three parallel branches after the events of Ocarina of Time - there's the Fallen Hero timeline (Link to the Past, Link's Awakening), the Child timeline (Majora's Mask and Twilight Princess), and the Adult timeline (Wind Waker, Phantom Hourglass).

But Nintendo has never placed too much importance on directly connecting these games one way or another, as series creator Shigeru Miyamoto never thought it was "that important." Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom's chronological placements have even been left a little ambiguous (they come after every other Zelda story falls into myth, sure, but are potentially set in an entirely separate Hyrule.) Needless to say, debates around the official timeline will likely persist as long as the series is still kicking.

"So, the timeline was in the back of our mind, but we didn't consider taking an approach to this game where we actually, explicitly included references like that," Aonuma adds.

Things might get even more complicated with more Zelda on the way next year, though Breath of the Wild director Hidemaro Fujibayashi thinks it's important that new Zelda games don't simply re-use older ideas.

Nintendo exec says "new games and remakes both have a place" following Zelda: Ocarina of Time launch

Gattuso a Lazio Style Channel: “Abbiamo sofferto con il Napoli, ma abbiamo avuto coraggio”

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Gennaro Gattuso, allenatore della Lazio, ha parlato ai microfoni di Lazio Style Channel dopo il pari contro il Napoli: “Siamo stati in trincea, potevamo perdere o vincere. Nel primo tempo potevamo fare più gol, nel secondo ci stava la loro reazione. Abbiamo sofferto, poi siamo tornati in partita. Potevamo fare scelte diverse, ho ancora in mente Noslin che calcia con Isaksen solo, la palla di Cancellieri appena entrato, è una mattonella che a Zaccagni piace e potevamo calciare meglio. Nel finale siamo stati là, potevamo attaccare meglio la profondità e siamo stati piatti. Ma mi piace il fatto che i ragazzi fossero delusi a fine partita, anche se non ci deve essere nessuna delusione: hanno dimostrato grande coraggio e mentalità. Siamo stati là con un primo tempo incredibile, poi abbiamo tenuto botta e siamo tornati in partita. Il Napoli ha giocatori forti, che hanno vinto scudetti e Champions. Siamo stati bravi, ci teniamo stretto il punto, ma mi è piaciuta la mentalità. Abbiamo fatto undici gare, ce ne sono ancora tante e dobbiamo dimostrare gara dopo gara. Non guardiamo Inter-Como, dobbiamo capire cosa stiamo facendo e seguire la strada della mentalità. Per un allenatore la cosa più bella è giocare qua a viso aperto, sapevamo che potevamo soffrire qualcosa ma anche loro faticavano a tenerci in alcune fasi. Abbiamo accettato il rischio e mi è piaciuto. Belahyane? È merito suo, ha cambiato atteggiamento. Quando nella vita capisci che la priorità per fare questo lavoro è metterci passione, voglia e appartenenza, allora cresci. Ci abbiamo lavorato, lui l’ha fatto come tanti altri. Taylor ha giocato mezzo zoppo, tanti atteggiamenti positivi per cui essere soddisfatti. Poi vedremo dove arriveremo, ma ci sono tantissime cose positive ed è merito di questi ragazzi”.

I Am Naples Testata Giornalistica - aut. El Shaddai: Zero Nine Reversi now available for PC

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

crim has released El Shaddai: Zero Nine Reversi, “a board game that puts a bold twist on classic Reversi,” for PC via Steam at a 10 percent-off launch discount price of $3.59. The price will change to $3.99 after November 15.

El Shaddai: Zero Nine Reversi first launched for iOS via App Store and Android via Google Play on April 27. The mobile versions were updated alongside the PC release today with online multiplayer support and an archive.

Here is an overview of the game, via its Steam page:

Zero Nine Reversi is a board game that puts a bold twist on classic Reversi. Every stone has HP.

The game includes two ways to play: “Classic,” a deep single-player challenge, and “Online,” where you pick a character and battle other players.

In normal Reversi, flipping a stone is the end of the story. In Zero Nine Reversi, a flipped stone loses 1 HP. When its HP reaches zero, the stone is purified and vanishes from the board. This simple change turns every match into a battle of attrition, where positioning, timing, and resource management matter as much as territory.

A versus mode where you choose a character and fight. On top of stone HP, it adds the “BUMP & BURST” rules and a unique ability for each character.
