Saving Mass Effect would take $200m+, says BioWare co-founder

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Anyone hoping to buy Mass Effect from EA and finish what BioWare started needs to get saving now, as it could cost over $200 million (£150m).

That's according to BioWare co-founder Trent Oster, who reckons that's what a new Mass Effect game would need to meet fans' expectations.

"The scary component of Mass Effect is the audience is going to want the best of 'AAA' development," Oster said on Twitter/X, as spotted by GamesRadar.

The scary component of Mass Effect is the audience is going to want the best of “AAA” development. Rough ballpark (based on contemporaries) is $200M+. That is a big number to commit up front. There has to be a bigger plan in place for the IP to make the math work.

"Rough ballpark (based on contemporaries) is $200M+. That is a big number to commit up front. There has to be a bigger plan in place for the IP to make the math work."

He did add that it could be done for less, though. "That said, I think it can be done well for less than that amount with a disciplined approach and an experienced team," Oster said.

"I think a game could be done cheaper, if you kept it simple and hit the big beats," he added in a separate tweet. "It wouldn’t be what the team described in the Universe concept. The big push would be getting the IP free. The fans deserve more than one game."

I think a game could be done cheaper, if you kept it simple and hit the big beats. It wouldn’t be what the team described in the Universe concept. The big push would be getting the IP free. The fans deserve more than one game.

Oster's comments come as the #SaveMassEffect campaign continues to grow following EA's decision to close BioWare and cancel Mass Effect 5. Fans want EA to keep making Mass Effect games, or hand the series over to a studio that will.

Oster has backed the campaign too. "I love seeing all the energy around #SaveMassEffect," he wrote on 4th November. "I think there is a still a great thesis for a well-funded RPG developer who can deliver. Makes me want to get some of the original band back together."

EA has not confirmed whether or not it would consider selling the IP.

The petition created to save BioWare's well-loved sci-fi RPG, Mass Effect, topped 185,000 signatures in just days. It's now exceeded 317,000. Joe Smith, credited with starting the petition, isn't necessarily asking EA to change its mind about BioWare; instead, the petition calls for "decisive action by committing to the continued development of Mass Effect".

We said a sad farewell to BioWare last month. The closure, which came just months after Saudi Arabia's $55bn leveraged buyout of EA, was anticipated by experts, but drew widespread condemnation and messages of sympathy from across the industry.

A new report claims EA closed BioWare because it was forced to choose between investing in one of two sci-fi projects: a new Mass Effect game or a new Star Wars Battlefront game. And it chose the latter.

Fans are trying other routes to keep BioWare's doors open, too. Canadian fans of the studio have escalated their grievances to the governmental level, calling on the Canadian Government to step in and save the studio from closure, seemingly after Canada's New Democratic Party leader Avi Lewis called on the government to do so.

How to Master the Run/Walk Method: Training Principles for Consistent Success

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Build a well-rounded run/walk routine with these tips from experts.

Whether you want to boost your health, run your first race, or set a new PR, the run/walk method makes those goals accessible for everyone of all fitness backgrounds and levels. But finding run/walk success goes well beyond determining your Magic Mile paces, tracking your intervals, and finishing your goal race.

By following some key training principles, you can make your run/walk regimen a fundamental part of your everyday routine. Runner’s World’s How to Master the Run/Walk Method program walks you through the essential guidance you need to stick with your training, stay healthy, and enjoy each step of your run/walk journey.

In Runner’s World’s How to Master the Run/Walk Method: Training Principles for Consistent Success, we’ll teach you:

As you continue with your run/walk routine and watch your training progress, keep the following training principles in mind for long-term run/walk success:

Practice makes perfect, right? Training on a regular basis—even when you’re not working toward a goal race—will help you reach your fitness aims and maintain your run/walk progress.

Now, that doesn’t mean you need to be in race training mode year round. Use one of Runner’s World’s maintenance plans to structure your off-season workouts and sustain your motivation. You can also use our four-week run/walk maintenance plan, which keeps your body prepped before heading into your next training cycle.

All of our run/walk training plans have a weekly rest day for a reason. The time you spend off your feet is when progress happens, allowing your body to soak up all of the hard work you’ve put in. Build in recovery time after your hard workouts and long runs, and also set aside a few minutes each day for recovery methods like stretching or foam rolling.

And don’t forget to catch those z’s! Sleep is one of—if not the—most important recovery tools you can use to your advantage for muscle and tissue repair, improved immune function, and injury prevention, among other benefits.

While it’s important to stay consistent with your running mileage, it’s also important to strength train. We recommend adding strength training into your routine one to three times a week to support stronger muscles, protect your joints, add force behind your stride, and become more resilient.

Aim for more strength workouts earlier in your training block. As your mileage and workout intensity increases throughout your plan, you can reduce your strength workout frequency.

Not sure where to begin with strength workouts? Use our Guide to Strength Training and Race-Ready Strength program to get started with your routine.

A smart nutrition strategy keeps you running strong, and is an especially important consideration as you start running longer distances like half marathons and marathons. As you increase your activity, your body needs more calories to support that movement.

To build your runner’s diet, prioritize whole foods, along with grains, fruits, vegetables, and lean proteins in your diet. Make sure to hydrate, both throughout your day and during your workouts, with water and electrolytes to replenish fluids.

Before long runs, eat enough carbohydrates, and get a good mix of carbs and protein into your system afterward. For runs longer than 60 minutes, bring fuel with you, like gels, gummies, electrolytes, and sports drinks.

For more on how to properly fuel your training and racing, dig into the Runner’s World Guide to Nutrition and Fueling, which includes tips from industry experts on hydration, midrun fueling, race day nutrition, and so much more.

Running is often seen as a solo sport—but it doesn’t have to be. Find a run club, virtual run/walk group, or a training partner to stay consistent with your training and have fun while doing so. Having other run/walkers supporting you helps you stay accountable, find meaning in your miles, and unlock even more enjoyment.

“The fact of the matter is running is a lot more fun when you’re doing it with other people,” says experienced run/walker Cheryl Munn. “I don’t think that I would’ve kept at it if I hadn’t joined a local running group.”

Ashley is Editor of Content Hype at Hearst’s Enthusiast & Wellness Group. She is a former collegiate runner at UNC Asheville where she studied mass communication. Ashley loves all things running; she has raced two marathons, plus has covered some of the sport’s top events in her career, including the Paris Olympics, U.S. Olympic Trials and multiple World Marathon Majors.

Mark Zuckerberg Meta AI Predicts ‘Will Crypto Recover in November’

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Bitcoin (BTC) is trading near $75,800–$76,000 and Ethereum (ETH) is around $2,230–$2,235 as of November 8, 2026. Mark Zuckerberg’s Meta AI predicts, ‘Will crypto recover in November? ‘, which is the question on many traders’ lips heading into a big week across the markets.

Bitcoin sits roughly 40% below its October 2025 peak of about $126,000, while Ethereum remains well off its own highs. The market is asking whether crypto can stage a meaningful recovery this month. After the post-midterm bounce, both assets have consolidated.

Bitcoin has held the mid-$70,000s after testing higher levels earlier in the recovery, while Ethereum continues to lag, trading with a relatively weak ETH/BTC ratio near 0.0295.

This is typical in the early stages of a potential recovery, when Bitcoin usually leads, which is why all eyes should be on BTC USD over the coming week.

Bitcoin has formed a series of higher lows since the mid-year troughs and is defending the $74,800–$75,500 zone. A weekly close back above $81,000–$82,000 would strengthen the case for a durable base.

A break below $74,000 would reopen downside risk toward the low $70,000s. Ethereum remains softer. It is struggling to reclaim key moving averages and needs a sustained move through $2,400–$2,500 to show relative strength returning.

Until the ETH/BTC ratio turns higher, a full “altcoin season” style recovery remains unlikely, but a shift in market conditions can quickly change sentiment.

CRYPTO THIS WEEKNovember 9–15, 2026A busy week across crypto markets, policy, institutions and blockchain infrastructure. Inflation, exchange and ecosystem updates will shape the week.THE HIGHLIGHTS:✧ China October inflation data arrives November 9.✧ US CPI is out… pic.twitter.com/26z29H08Aa

The most immediate event is the October CPI report on Tuesday, November 10. Consensus expects a cooling in the monthly rate (around +0.2–0.3% headline). A softer-than-expected print would ease rate-hike fears, support risk assets, and give Bitcoin and Ethereum room to push higher into the second half of the month.

A hotter reading would reinforce the “higher-for-longer” narrative, pressure yields higher, and likely stall or reverse the recent rebound.

Soft inflation and resilient but not overheating growth data would be constructive. Sticky inflation or stronger-than-expected economic prints that raise December rate-hike odds would act as a headwind. Spot Bitcoin ETF flows remain the marginal driver.

Recent weekly outflows have weighed on price, so a return to consistent net inflows would be an important confirmation of recovering institutional demand.

Stablecoin growth and on-chain activity continue to favor Ethereum over the longer term, but near-term price action is still dominated by macro and Bitcoin leadership.

A modest recovery is possible if the November 10 CPI comes in soft and ETF flows stabilize. In that scenario, Bitcoin could retest the $82,000–$85,000 area and Ethereum could push back toward $2,400–$2,500 by month-end.

A hotter inflation print or continued ETF outflows would likely keep the market range-bound or push prices back toward recent lows. A full-blown bull-market breakout to new all-time highs is unlikely in November.

The more realistic path is a grinding recovery that builds through the end of 2026 and into 2027, assuming inflation continues to cool and institutional demand returns.

For now, the November CPI print is the single most important near-term catalyst that will decide whether the post-midterm bounce has legs or fades.

Former writers, composers, directors, and more celebrate N7 Day

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Every year, N7 Day (November 7th) sees fans celebrate and reminisce on the fantastic Mass Effect series, but this year's milestone is a little different - it's the first without developer BioWare, which was recently closed at the hands of parent company EA. And as expected, love from former developers was pouring in from all sides.

Original Mass Effect game director Casey Hudson (who also led development on Star Wars: KOTOR) is pretty hopeful for the sci-fi universe's future. "Doesn't feel right to say 'happy' N7Day this year, but I'm optimistic that Mass Effect will live on! When Mass Effect makes its triumphant return, it will be thanks to the outpouring of love from players around the world," he tweets. "Thank you for sharing what it means to you."

Mass Effect 5 - or Mass Effect Universe - project lead Michael Gamble gave a big thank you to fans over on social media: "You all are truly amazing. The love and passion for this…universe…is incredible. Even in worst days, we could always count on you. To all the stories left untold. Happy N7 Day."

The original trilogy's composer Sam Hulick reveals that he had actually been chatting to BioWare about returning to write music for Mass Effect 5, via a new blog, though nothing had been set in stone. To celebrate N7 Day, Hulick's blog post also includes a re-scored version of the leaked Mass Effect Universe trailer - those bouncing dark synths at the end are just chef's kiss.

"I knew BioWare wanted to do something new with the music, and I wanted to be conscious about not just repeating the same formula used in the original trilogy," the composer says. "Fans really loved the ME1 end-credits song by Faunts, and I thought it would've been incredible to bring more songs with lyrics into the game. This would’ve been a great way to highlight more emotional moments in the narrative."

Former BioWare narrative designer Michael Tucker tweets that working on his favorite series ever was the "chance of a lifetime" and the experience was "nothing less than a dream come true."

"I'm proud of the work we did. I'm proud that as a fellow fan, every decision I got to make was aimed directly at delivering an unforgettable experience for the Mass Effect fans. What a gift it was to have that be my job for five years," he writes.

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

Mass Effect: Annihilation novelist and one of the writers on the cancelled Mass Effect 5, Catherynne M. Valente, published a pretty substantial blog post for N7 Day. "I've lost count of how many times I've typed the word 'devastated' over the last two weeks," she writes. "Maybe that sounds silly. You can call it 'just a video game' all you want, but it will never, ever be just a video game to me."

Ex-BioWare animator Jonathan Cooper even released a rare, "very pre-alpha internal trailer intended to highlight the cinematics" of the original trilogy games. It's embedded above and worth a watch.

Some of My Favorite Evergreen Shrubs Just Went on Sale at Home Depot – and They'll Keep Your Garden Looking Good Year-Round

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Beautiful plants and ideas to brighten your garden.

Expert tips for growing fruit, vegetables and herbs at home.

Sign up to get fresh gardening ideas, inspiration and expert advice delivered straight to your inbox.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

All-year backyard structure on a budget? Yes please! Evergreen shrubs are the landscape-design must every garden is missing, and these can all be planted now.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

As leaves drop in fall, I always wish I’d planted more evergreen shrubs in my garden to give structure and color all through the winter. From tall arborvitae cones to slender columns of holly and perfect no-prune spheres, evergreens give a garden a shapely skeleton on which to hang seasonal blooms. While I’m lucky enough to have mature yew hedges and an old conifer I’m planning to cloud-prune, I want to add evergreen accents to borders and alongside a path to punctuate my garden design. Globes of green leaves in patio planters are high on my wish list too.

What's stopped me? One of the basic principles of garden design is to always repeat-plant evergreens in odd numbers – and bying multiple plants gets expensive, fast. But Home Depot must have been listening in to my garden-design dreams because it’s not even Thanksgiving yet and a heap of my favorite evergreen shrubs just had their prices slashed in an early Black Friday plant sale. And frankly, the prices were pretty decent even before they went on sale.

Let’s skip straight to the good bit, shall we?

If you’re thinking about planting a privacy screen or hedge, look no further than Emerald Green arborvitae. It naturally grows into a narrow pyramid, reaching 10-14 feet high but only 2-4 feet wide, so you don’t lose too much yardspace or sunlight but get dense foliage to screen your garden from prying neighbors. While many evergreens fade in the coldest winter months, this variety is well known for keeping its bright, glossy tone year round. Thuja occidentalis ‘Smagard’ puts on about 6-12 inches of growth a year so while you won’t get privacy overnight, you won’t need to spend half your weekend pruning it forever more either. Suitable for zones 3-8.

While most boxwood shrubs grow incredibly slowly, sprinter boxwood lives up to its name, meaning you can buy a smaller, cheaper plant. And don’t worry that Buxus microphylla will grow too big because it maxes out at 2-4 feet high and wide: large enough to have an impact in your yard without taking over. Sprinter boxwood keeps its glossy green foliage year-round and has excellent resistance to diseases and pests, including boxwood blight. Suitable for zones 4-9, this handsome shrub is easily shaped to create structural evergreen mounds in a border, a low square hedge or appealing spheres in planters.

Thuja occidentalis ‘Anna van Vloten’ is a dwarf evergreen that naturally grows in a compact sphere without ever needing to be clipped. With wonderfully textural fan-like foliage, it keeps a bright golden-green tone year-round and grows well in zones 3-8. Anna’s Magic Ball arborvitae only grows to 10-15 inches high and wide and looks delightful dotted through a border. If you’re looking for compact shrubs to boost your curb appeal or want to add some stylish year-round planters to your patio, here’s the easy solution.

Here’s another dwarf cultivar of Thuja occidentalis that naturally grows in a compact ball shape without requiring any pruning. Danica America arborvitae is a little bigger at 2 feet high and wide and a brighter green, but what really sets it apart is its fan-like foliage that grows in vertical layers. The evergreen foliage is denser than Anna’s Magic Ball, too. Use it to create a smart geometric accent alongside a path or add stylish structure to a border without taking up too much space. It's one of the best evergreens for containers, too, and suitable for zones 3-7.

For instant style in zones 6-9, few evergreen shrubs have the striking aplomb of sky pencil holly. It’s a cultivar of the lovely Japanese holly and while it grows 6-10 feet tall, it stays a slender 1-2 feet wide with unusual vertical branches. The leaves are small, glossy and dark green – more like a boxwood than a holly – so the growth is dense, accentuating the shape. So, while sky pencil holly doesn’t boast red berries (though it does grow small black berries in fall), its silhouette provides more than enough drama. Happy in a large planter or in well-drained ground, sky pencil holly looks fabulous either side of a doorway or gate, or planted in a loose line, and is one of the best shrubs for a front yard.

So far, so green. And while it’s wonderful to have shrubs that bring texture and a year-round skeleton of leaves, some evergreen shrubs also deliver a generous splash of color. And Encore azalea is one of the very best flowering plants to add to your garden in fall. While many azaleas are deciduous, and older varieties only flower for a few short weeks in spring, the Encore series not only keeps its leaves through winter but repeat-blooms all through spring, summer and autumn. It maintains a tidy, rounded shape so requires very little pruning, and there are all sorts of flower colors to choose from. Suitable for zones 6-10, Autumn Royalty has bright magenta blooms and you’ll also find Autumn Lily with white flowers, Autumn Carnation with pink flowers, and Autumn Embers with red-orange flowers in Home Depot’s sale.

Euonymus fortunei ‘Roemertwo’ is one of those easy wins that every garden needs, requiring little looking after but working hard four seasons of the year. Don't be put off by the fact it's a wintercreeper as this cultivar is a compact mounding shrub. Its evergreen foliage doesn’t lose its variegation and revert to green like many shrubs, and thank goodness because the lime-green leaves have lovely gold margins that positively glow on a dark winter day. Adding to the color, Gold Splash wintercreeper boasts yellow fruit that open to reveal orange seeds in fall. Highly resistant to disease, this neat, ultra-tough shrub reaches 18-24 inches high and wide in zones 5-8.

Okay, so Viburnum nudum ‘Brandywine’ isn’t evergreen but it does look good all through winter on account of the multi-colored berries that cling to the branches months after leaves have fallen. Brandywine viburnum is one of the best berry-bearing shrubs to feel birds through winter as its berries only sweeten once the worst winter weather sets in, providing a valuable late-season pantry for our feathered friends. Those berries provide quite a show, too, starting green then shifting through pink and blue to a glossy dark purple. Hardy in zones 5-9, this low-maintenance shrub reaches 5-6 feet high.

Emma is an avid gardener and has worked in media for over 25 years. Previously editor of Modern Gardens magazine, she regularly writes for the Royal Horticultural Society. She loves to garden hand-in-hand with nature and her garden is full of bees, butterflies and birds as well as cottage-garden blooms. As a keen natural crafter, her cutting patch and veg bed are increasingly being taken over by plants that can be dried or woven into a crafty project.

9 fast fixes to help you stop doomscrolling

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 8, 2026 2:11 PM EST

Doomscrolling: To spend an inordinate amount of time swiping up and down across multiple social media and news feeds—without really getting anything of use out of it. In fact, as addictive as it is, doomscrolling often leaves us feeling worse about ourselves and the world than when we started.

You might find yourself engaging in doomscrolling right before you go to bed, first thing after waking up, standing in line on the subway platform, or even—this is how bad it’s gotten—sitting at the dinner table with the family.

It doesn’t have to be this way though. There are tools and tactics you can use to beat doomscrolling and spend less time on your phone—which means more time enjoying the world and the people around you (and catching up on your sleep). Here are nine tips that can help, across all makes and models of phone.

A grayscale interface can make your phone less distracting. On Android, open Settings and pick Digital Wellbeing > Bedtime Mode to automatically switch to grayscale at a certain time, or enable it manually via Accessibility > Color and motion > Color correction.

On an iPhone, open Settings then choose Accessibility > Display & Text Size > Color Filters to find the grayscale option. There’s no automatic evening color drain like there is on Android, but you can automate the switch by using the Shortcuts app built into iOS.

Both Android and iOS now include tools for setting limits on the time you can spend inside particular apps each day. You can of course easily choose to disable these limits whenever you like, but it might still go some way to weaning you off your doomscrolling habits.

On Android, from Settings head to Digital Wellbeing > App timers to start choosing your apps and your time limits. Over on iOS, from Settings it’s Screen Time > App & Website Activity to do the same. You could even try slowly reducing your limits over time.

You might not have noticed, but there are now a wealth of apps to help wean you off unhealthy phone habits, and make sure you keep on top of your screen time. They all gamify the process in some way, encouraging and rewarding you for not doomscrolling.

Some of our favorites include Forest (for Android and iOS), Focus Friend (for Android and iOS), and Focus Traveller (for iOS): They use growing trees, helping a bean character, and hiking up a mountain, respectively, to help you stay on task and avoid distractions.

Pause Point is a new feature available now in Android 17 on Pixel devices, and potentially rolling out more widely in the future. You can find it by opening Settings and choosing Digital Wellbeing > Pause Point, then tapping Continue to get the feature configured.

For the apps that you choose, every time you open them up, you get one of your selected pause points. They include breathing exercises, and custom photo galleries, and the idea is to take a beat and consider if you really need to open up that distracting app after all.

Sometimes the apps and options on your phone aren’t enough on their own. If that’s the case, try the Brick: It’s a $59 physical dongle that uses NFC tech, and when you tap your phone against it, it enables a custom quiet mode that blocks certain websites and apps.

The idea is that the Brick essentially bricks your phone for certain periods, so you spend less time on it. It’s not the only gadget that does this trick either—you could also check out the Autonomous Key, which can be yours for a mere $19 and works along similar lines.

Both Android and iOS let you use so-called Focus modes to cut down on distractions and reduce the temptation to doomscroll. They work like a Do Not Disturb mode with extras, and you can configure different settings for sleeping, driving, or trying to get work done.

On Android, open Settings and then head to Modes; on iOS, it’s Settings and then Focus. The features work in a similar way on both platforms, letting you edit an existing Focus mode or start your own from scratch, with exemptions for certain apps and contacts.

Certain apps come with their own features for limiting the amount of time you spend in them, so it’s worth having a look through the apps that cause you to doomscroll the most to see what options you’ve got—even if it’s just dialing down the number of notifications.

Instagram is one example. Open up the profile tab, then tap the menu button (three horizontal lines) and pick Time management. From the next screen you can set yourself a daily limit, and enable a sleep mode (when notifications are muted) for specific times.

This is just for the Samsung Galaxy owners out there, and if your phone has the latest One UI 9 software installed on it, there’s a new tool available in it called AI Network Guard. It’s designed for kids, but it works well for keeping adults (i.e. you) off distracting apps too.

It’s well hidden though: From Settings, tap Connections > Wi-Fi, then the three dots in the corner. Then, tap Intelligent Wi-Fi five times to bring up the Connectivity labs entry. Here you’ll find AI Network Guard, and can choose the apps to impose usage restrictions on.

This is a classic, because the fewer notifications you get, the less likely you are to pick up your phone. There are now a host of ways to tweak alerts on both Android and iOS, but essentially it’s about limiting how and when your apps can interrupt what you’re doing.

On Android or iOS, choose Notifications from Settings to see the options open to you. You can make alerts less distracting or disable them altogether, on an app-by-app basis, and access useful features like notification snoozing (Android) or scheduled summaries (iOS).

5 Yoga Poses to Strengthen Your Mid Back (Yes, Your Mid Back)

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

If you’re seeking ways to strengthen your back, chances are you won’t find a lot of solutions for how to strengthen your mid back. Yoga teacher and certified personal trainer Amber Sayer, who works with desk workers as well as competitive athletes, explains why the missing mid-back component is so essential and how to effectively strengthen it.

When it comes to strength training and your back, almost everyone talks about thinks about avoiding lower back strain or correcting tech neck. But there isn’t much talk about how to strengthen and improve endurance in the muscles of the mid back.

And that’s a shame. This overlooked part of the body not only contributes to your ease of posture and movement during everyday life, it ensures that all of the muscles in your back can work together effectively. Basically, when any part of the back is underutilized, it creates more work for the other parts. Practicing exercises that challenge and build endurance in your mid back supports functional back strength throughout the spine and helps minimize the risk of forcing the muscles in the lower back to overcompensate. Think of your mid-back muscles as one of those things you don’t tend to think about until there’s a problem.

These muscles attach to the thoracic spine and include the middle and lower trapezius, rhomboids, latissimus dorsi, erector spinae, and serratus posterior. You’re probably already been practicing yoga poses that strengthen the muscles in the mid back in various ways without realizing it. But when you become more intentional about including these poses each time you’re on your mat, you ensure that you target the various muscles and collaboratively, strengthen your entire back.

Rather than practicing the entire list of yoga poses all at once, incorporate a couple into your mat time whenever you practice, occasionally changing up which ones you explore. Or try a single pose as a one-off whenever you could use a respite from sitting and staring at your screen.

This beginner-friendly pose is your starting point as you’re learning to strengthen the mid-back muscles. Because Sphinx Pose (Salamba Bhujangasana) props you on your forearms to create the backbend, it can be almost entirely passive for the back muscles. But there are a few subtle actions you can take that engage the mid-back in significant yet non-intense ways.

How to: Lie on your belly with your forehead on the mat and your legs straight behind you, hip distance apart, with the tops of your feet on the mat. Bring your palms and forearms on the mat with your elbows beneath or slightly in front of your shoulders. Actively press down through your forearms to lift your chest off the mat. Draw your shoulder blades down and away from your ears and toward the middle of your back. And the important part for your mid-back is to keep your forearms still but feel as if you’re dragging your elbows toward your hips. Keep that action as you stay here for several breaths.

If the backbend feels too intense, inch your forearms slightly in front of you to lessen how much your chest is lifted from the mat.

This backbend asks you to lift your chest off the mat by pressing your palms to the mat. But when you hover your hands in Cobra Pose (Bhujangasana), you rely exclusively on your mid-back and core muscles to create the shape. The point isn’t how far you lift yourself off the mat. It’s that you create any lift at all.

How to: Start lying on your belly with your palms beneath your shoulders. Start with your palms pressing into the mat and lift your chest. Then press the tops of your feet into the mat and engage your mid-back muscles to lift your chest any amount off the mat. To intensify the workload on your mid-back muscles, lift your palms an inch or two above the mat and continue to hover them. You can also bring your arms straight out from your shoulders like the letter T, and then turn your thumbs toward the ceiling to increase the strengthening potential for the trapezius.

This shape asks you to actively lift your upper and lower body off the mat, which enlists the mid-back muscles as you counteract gravity. It effectively strengthens not only the mid back but the entire back body, including the upper and lower back, glutes, and hamstrings. Locust Pose (Salabhasana) is not easy but it is one of the best back-strengthening yoga poses that exist.

How to: Start lying on your belly with your arms at your sides, palms facing down. Press down through the fronts of the hips and start to lift your upper and lower body off the mat, drawing the shoulder blades toward each other. Lift only as much as you can. Interlace your fingers behind your back and reach your knuckles toward the wall behind you.

This pose is commonly thought of as a quad-strengthening yoga pose, as that’s usually where you feel the burn. But leaning forward in Chair Pose (Utkatasana) without completely collapsing forward and reaching your arms overhead also challenges—and strengthens—the muscles of the mid back. Specifically, the lower trapezius and erector spinae.

How to: From standing, bring your big toes to touch or keep them hip-distance apart. Bend your knees and sit your hips partly back, as if you’re about to sit in a chair. Lean your chest slightly forward and reach your arms alongside your ears. Draw your shoulders away from your ears to strengthen the trapezius and rhomboids.

To intensify the challenge for your your mid back, take any of the following arm variations: • Take your hands farther away from one another to create a V shape with your arms and squeeze your shoulder blades together • Bend your elbows about 90 degrees and create a cactus shape with your arms, being careful not to arch your lower back • Sweep your arms alongside your body, palms facing the mat, drawing your shoulder blades away from your ears

This Warrior pose tends to be less common than Warrior 1 or 2 when it comes to most yoga strength routines, yet it is an unsung hero in terms of strengthening not only the legs but most of the muscles in your body. The trick for targeting the mid-back muscles is to reach your straightened arms in front of you with your torso in Warrior 3 (Virabhadrasana III).

How to: From standing with your hands on your hips or at your chest, shift your weight into one foot and slowly start to lean forward as you lift the other foot and reach it toward the wall behind you. Slowly reach your arms straight ahead, with your arms alongside your ears. Alternatively, you can reach them alongside your body, with your fingertips reaching toward the back wall, in what’s commonly referred to as airplane arms. Imagine actively lifting your straightened arms up toward the ceiling.

Day 31: The Concert From the Castle

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

With the moon still plump and full, I set out through the mountains from the restful town I’d slept in.

It was daylight, and as I carefully descended the path strewn with loose rocks, I listened to the book that taught me about the Road to Santiago.

In the town before my destination, I planned to stop and eat breakfast. The cafe cats caught my attention as I approached. As the whole street came into view, I saw my Italian brother smiling at me. I ran to him and gave him a hug.

We always managed to stumble upon each other without planning, and I loved it.

When we made it to the city of Ponferrada, we went directly to the donativo albergue—San Nicolás de Flüe Pilgrims Hostel—to wait for it to open. The courtyard was large, with many trees, a large wooden totem, and a vine-covered sitting area. I went into the church nearby looking for a restroom. What I found, though, made me take a seat in the pew.

Within those tiny walls, an extraordinary mural was painted on the ceiling, depicting scenes from the Camino de Santiago, the four cardinal directions, the elements, and the stars. It stirred both the artist and the spiritual side within me.

When I went back to where we were relaxing, our Argentine brother had arrived. The three of us called ourselves “Familia Real”, the name emphasizing the bond we had created.

Once again, without planning, we were all together.

As a religious donativo albergue, I was placed on the woman’s side of the building. When I saw the name of the room I’d been assigned to, I couldn’t help but smile. “Cruz del Ferro.”

That place would forever hold a special place in my heart.

The brothers and I went on a side quest to a bodega overlooking the Castle of the Knights Templar.

After the siesta hours ended, I went to the Castle of the Knights Templar to embark on the tourist part of the day. I’d made it a few feet into the castle walkway when I saw my pilgrim friend below on the street. He was a musician who carried a flute with him everywhere, taking full advantage to play and sing and jam at every opportunity. I yelled down, asking him to play, but that was already his plan.

A man he met in the city joined him with a violin and I was entranced.

Standing on a castle wall, listening to music from artists I knew, with the city and mountains stretching out behind them, felt surreal.

That moment will forever be stamped into my memory.

After each song ended, I clapped from my perch on the castle wall, and the pilgrim smiled, skipping around with pride. His passion for music poured out of him with such a burning fire, and I felt lucky to witness it.

I knew I had a lot more to see, though, so I went on my way.

Exploring the exterior, I went level after level, corridor after corridor. I don’t like big houses, but now I want to live in a castle.

The secret passageways and rooms reminded me of the forts I used to build as a child.

And being on top of that tower overlooking the castle, the city, and the mountains?

I learned more about medieval culture than I ever had in my life. I explored an exhibit of a collection of books. The texts on display were all shapes and sizes, some as thick as my head, some smaller than a deck of cards.

Had it not been for exhaustion hitting me, I could’ve stayed for several more hours.

After 2.5 hours, though, I knew it was time to move on.

A grocery run on my way back to the albergue, a simple dinner in the chaotic kitchen, then quality time with my brothers.

The day had mirrored the life I’d grown accustomed to on the Camino.

In the kitchen at the end of the night, I met a fellow American for the first time. He’d seen me before in a different town and started the conversation with,

“You were working on your laptop and all I could think was ‘damn, she must be writing a book.’”

She fell in love with long walks in nature during her Appalachian Trail 2022 thruhike where she dove into finding herself through the space that the trail provided. Since completing her flip-flop at Springer Mountain, she has crafted her lifestyle to travel full-time and serve others within her passion of holistic health. This Bitcoin Developer’s Idea Would Make Quantum Attacks a Losing Bet

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

This Bitcoin Developer’s Idea Would Make Quantum Attacks a Losing Bet

Bitcoin developer Cory Fields has an unusual idea for dealing with future quantum thieves: Make them risk their own Bitcoin every time they try to steal someone else’s.

In a November 4 post to the Bitcoin development mailing list, Fields outlined “quantum landmines” and two types of auctions designed to turn attacks on vulnerable wallets into expensive gambles. He called the ideas “thought experiments,” not a completed Bitcoin upgrade.

Bitcoin owners use a secret private key to authorize transactions. A matching public key lets the network check their signatures without learning the secret.

If the whole idea seems too hard to understand, we used AI to generate a video to help you grasp it:

Today’s computers cannot realistically calculate that private key from the public one. A sufficiently powerful quantum computer could use a mathematical technique called Shor’s algorithm to do it, potentially letting a thief spend coins as if they owned them.

Some older Bitcoin outputs have exposed public keys, and certain newer formats reveal them too. Other addresses hide the key until someone spends, so the risk is not identical for every wallet.

Researchers call the hypothetical arrival of a computer capable of these attacks “Q-Day.” No publicly demonstrated machine can steal Bitcoin this way today, and nobody knows exactly when one might exist.

Imagine someone owns 1 BTC in an old, potentially vulnerable wallet. Before Q-Day, this person creates a tiny, separate quantum-safe Bitcoin output containing a hidden proof that they controlled the original private key while quantum theft was still impossible.

Think of it as a sealed ownership receipt. Outsiders can see the small output, but not the evidence tucked inside it, or even that it is a trap.

Now imagine a future thief uses a quantum computer to recover the user’s old private key. Under Fields’s suggested rules, the thief cannot simply take her 1 BTC: They must add 1 BTC of their own from a quantum-safe wallet as collateral.

Both coins enter a restricted output worth 2 BTC. The thief must wait through a challenge period—30 days in Fields’s example—before spending it freely.

The wallet owner notices the transaction and reveals the pre-Q-Day proof. The network then allows the owner to move the full 2 BTC to the quantum-safe address they committed to earlier: their original coin, plus the thief’s collateral.

The attacker has no way to know in advance which old wallets contain these hidden receipts. Even a successful quantum key recovery could become a 1 BTC loss.

The wallet owner would also need matching collateral to initiate a normal post-Q-Day move of her own legacy coin. If they have no spare BTC, the same rule intended to hurt thieves could make moving her savings difficult.

Landmines only work when an owner prepares one before Q-Day and reveals it in time. They cannot protect coins whose keys are already lost, including some of the early Bitcoin holdings widely believed to be inaccessible.

Fields proposes a second mechanism called a staked auction. Before Q-Day, someone could lock up quantum-safe Bitcoin and secretly register a bid against a specific old wallet they expect thieves to target.

Suppose an apparently abandoned wallet holds 50 BTC. A future attacker tries to move it and posts another 50 BTC as collateral; during the auction period, someone who committed a bid in advance can challenge the attempted theft.

In Fields’s example, a successful challenger claims part of the attacker’s collateral while the original vulnerable coins are destroyed rather than awarded to the bidder. The point is to deprive thieves of a reliable payday, not return the coins to a missing owner.

His third idea, a sats auction, requires no advance stake. Instead, a vulnerable-coin spend would trigger a contest in which the highest bidder gets the coins minus their bid, while the runner-up pays their bid and gets nothing.

That resembles a notorious auction game where two bidders keep raising their offers because neither wants to be the person who loses money for nothing. A thief might avoid starting a contest that could cost more than the coins they hoped to steal.

One proposed response to quantum risk is to restrict spending from outdated wallets after a migration deadline. That might prevent theft, but could also permanently lock out legitimate owners who never moved their coins.

Fields is exploring a different trade-off: Keep old coins potentially spendable, but make attempted transfers costly and open to challenges. Bitcoin cannot tell an honest owner from a quantum thief if both can produce the same valid signature.

The trade-off is severe, considering that honest owners might need large collateral deposits, inactive holders could miss their chance to object, and auction rules might burn coins belonging to people who are still alive and simply offline.

Bitcoin would first need a quantum-safe way to hold and spend funds. Wallets would then need to support hidden ownership commitments, while Bitcoin’s consensus rules would have to enforce collateral, waiting periods, special recovery transactions, and auctions.

Developers would also need to agree on when the new rules activate, how much collateral is required, and what happens to losing bids or leftover funds. Fields believes some mechanics could be introduced through a soft fork, a type of Bitcoin rule change, but has not supplied the detailed design.

None of these protections exists in Bitcoin today. Fields has published no implementation or activation schedule, and his original post leaves the auction payouts and even the desired outcome of an auction unresolved.

Trail Camera Photos of the First Coyotes to Use the World’s Largest Wildlife Crossing

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Trail Camera Photos of the First Coyotes to Use the World’s Largest Wildlife Crossing

The world’s largest wildlife crossing, the $114 million Wallis Annenberg Wildlife Crossing, hasn’t officially “opened,” but that hasn’t stopped the animals from using it above the extremely busy Highway 101 in Agoura Hills, California.

On October 28, a coyote became the first large mammal — a lizard was the first across the line, technically — to use the wildlife bridge. Yesterday, a second coyote went on it.

They were both caught on recently installed remote wildlife camera traps, an incredible tool for scientists and award-winning wildlife photographers alike.

As SFGate reports, the Wallis Annenberg Wildlife Crossing, which has been in the works since the National Wildlife Federation (NWF) and Santa Monica Mountains Fund began raising money for it in 2014, isn’t slated to have its ribbon-cutting ceremony until early December. Construction is ongoing, so it isn’t even technically a crossing yet.

Wildlife doesn’t wait. The project, which includes two bridges crossing the 10-lane Ventura Freeway, includes 13 acres of habitat restoration. Birds and bugs have already enjoyed the growing reclaimed habitat above the busy highway, while a western fence lizard was the first to go on the bridge’s surface itself.

The two coyotes were the first large mammals spotted on the structure itself, but a mountain lion and her two cubs, a mule deer, and bobcats have all been spied walking next to the wildlife crossing.

“To everyone who wondered how wildlife would know where to go without signs… apparently, they’ve figured it out,” writes the Facebook account for P22 Mountain Lion of Hollywood, an influential mountain lion that was tragically euthanized in 2022. The famous big cat made a lasting impact on California’s wildlife conservation efforts, inspiring many to get involved and help make projects like the Wallis Annenberg Wildlife Crossing possible.

For scientists, the new wildlife crossing bridge offers many interesting things to learn.

“This is more than just about whether animals use the crossing,” said Seth Riley from the National Park Service’s Santa Monica Mountains National Recreation Area team. “We want to understand how effective it is in reconnecting an ecosystem that has been divided by the freeway for decades. That is a much bigger scientific question and answering it will take years.”

Riley and other team members will learn a lot from cameras set up on and near the wildlife crossing, and the public will get to see many of these photos. For security reasons, there won’t be real-time public feeds from the site, though. Scientists will study how the wildlife crossing affects wildlife diversity, genetic diversity, habitat evolution, how light and sound affect animals, and more.

It’s an exciting time to be a wild animal near Highway 101. And a decidedly safer one.

Image creditsNational Wildlife Federation, Wallis Annenberg Wildlife Crossing

The Business Platinum Card® from American Express

As High As 300,000 points. Find Out Your Offer.

As High As 175,000 points. Find Out Your Offer.

Earlier this year, I was in the middle of a United Airlines status challenge, and I saw an offer for a new credit card with a nice welcome offer that I couldn't resist. I hadn't really played too deeply in the United ecosystem and didn't have any of Chase's United-branded credit cards.

Interestingly, I had a United Airlines debit card in the early 2000's, but that was closed on me when the U.S. passed the Durbin Amendment as part of the Dodd-Frank Wall Street Reform and Consumer Protection Act of 2010.

That locked many of us out of lucrative points earning on debit cards.

Anyway, I was excited when I was able to open the (see rates and fees).

Of course, I quickly earned the generous 90,000-mile and 3,000 PQP welcome bonus after spending $4,000 on purchases in the first three months of account opening and adding an authorized user (no longer available), but I was just as excited to learn I had many perks that came with the card, which I've continued to enjoy this year.

Currently, new cardholders can earn up to 90,000 bonus miles and 500 PQPs: earn 500 PQPs after your first purchase and 80,000 bonus miles after spending $4,000 on purchases in the first three months from account opening, plus, earn 10,000 bonus miles after adding an authorized user in the first three months from account opening.

Here is what comes with the card, which pleasantly surprised me after I got it.

One thing I didn't realize when I signed up for the card was that cardholders get a $200 United travel credit. That came in handy when I needed to book a recent flight to Montana to see my father.

I used the card's $200 annual United TravelBank cash to offset the $488 round-trip ticket cost for my flight from Newark Liberty International Airport (EWR) to Bozeman International Airport (BZN). So that trip cost me just $288. Score.

While I haven't used it yet, this card includes up to $150 in hotel credits each year at Renowned Hotels and Resorts for United® Cardmembers. I'm going to try to get a stay in this year to earn that valuable credit.

Here's a potential option that Chase has listed in Bozeman. I stayed at the Kimpton Armory Hotel Bozeman by IHG when it first opened, and it would be a fun hotel to return to this year.

The Quest card also comes with an up to $150 JSX flight credit each anniversary year, up to $180 in Instacart credits each calendar year (through Dec. 31, 2027), and up to $80 ($40 each) in rental car credits each anniversary year for your first and second Avis or Budget rental when you book through cars.united.com. I haven't used any of those perks yet.

I have used the ride-hailing statement credits a few times. You get up to $100 in ride-hailing statement credits with this card (up to $8 monthly from January through November and up to $12 in December; yearly activation required). So far this year, I've used it five times, saving $40.

Finally, there's also a $120 Global Entry, TSA PreCheck or Nexus credit every four years.

Related: United card bonuses: Best-ever offers on 2 of the personal cards

The Quest card comes with some pretty good mileage multipliers. You get 10 total miles per dollar spent on eligible United flights and 4 miles per dollar spent on other eligible United purchases (like onboard food). You also get 2 miles per dollar spent on dining, select streaming services and all other travel.

Note that your mileage earning on Basic Economy and base option tickets may differ from the Quest's standard 10 total miles per dollar spent rate.

I found the miles really added up when I was going for the minimum spend to earn my welcome bonus.

The card also comes with 1,000 bonus Premier qualifying points (PQPs) each year that can give you a head start on earning United elite status. While I was completing my minimum spend, I ended up qualifying for United Silver status, which comes with things like extra-legroom seats starting 24 hours before your flight.

I earned 3,000 PQPs when I opened the card (The current welcome offer includes 500 PQPs after your first purchase). That gave me a huge push toward the 6,000 PQPs I needed for Silver status.

I was able to use my Silver status on that same trip to Montana when I picked Economy Plus seats at check-in.

My Silver status also gives me the possibility for complimentary upgrades, available 24 hours before departure (though they are very few and far between for Silver-level elite status holders).

One of the most incredible perks of holding the Quest card is that it gives you access to additional Saver-level award availability and a discount of at least 10% on award flights.

I was able to spend just 85,000 miles for a one-way flight from Tokyo to Chicago and then on to Newark. That flight could have cost someone without status and the Quest card as much as 450,000 United miles.

In fact, United says my card has saved me 165,000 miles this year alone already.

The Quest card also gets you priority boarding in Group 2, which can mean the difference between being able to bring your carry-on bags onboard or having to gate check them. It also comes with two free checked bags and an annual 10,000-mile award flight discount starting on your first card anniversary.

While I don't love the $350 annual fee on the card, it's partially offset by the generous $200 United travel credit I receive each year. The annual 10,000-mile discount to use toward an eligible award flight is also worth at least $200 to me with my award booking patterns. Together, these two perks alone offset the card's annual fee.

I was mostly interested in the generous welcome bonus when I opened the United Quest Card, but I've been pleasantly surprised by how much additional value I've gotten from it. From the $200 United travel credit to the elite-status boost and the huge savings on award flights, the card has easily earned back the $350 annual fee for me in value.

The Quest card has turned out to be a real winner and will keep a place in my wallet going forward.

14 Everyday Home Items You Need to Replace More Often Than You Think

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

These overlooked swaps can make your home cleaner, safer, and fresher.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Your home might be a showcase of timeless design, with stylish accessories, custom artwork, and high-end furniture. But when the everyday things that keep a household functioning are left unattended to for long enough, the space may be less clean and organized (and safe!) than it looks.

“Even the most gorgeous spaces lose their charm if we forget to update essentials,” says Linda Hayslett, principal designer at LH.Designs. “Keeping your home looking and feeling fresh isn’t just about design—it’s also about the little things.”

Caring for your home means looking beyond the interiors. Giving neglected areas the attention they need can keep germs and grime at bay, and replacing certain items can also protect your health and safety. Consider your carbon monoxide alarm: If an aging unit fails during an emergency, the consequences could be deadly.

To help you keep everything in good shape, we asked experts which 14 household items you should replace as soon as possible. Take a look—you can thank us later!

Smoke and carbon monoxide detectors lose their sensitivity over time, which could delay detection during an emergency. The timeframe for when you should replace these will vary by manufacturer. Look up the model you have online to be sure—even a fairly new alarm should be tested once a month. The batteries should be replaced once a year.

Mold and mildew can build up on shower liners, especially in damp environments. A 2024 study by SafeHome.org showed that shower curtains had the most germs of any other surfaces in the bathroom, including the toilet seat. While Hayslett recommends washing them every three months, the material can eventually become brittle over time.

Dingy towels are the antithesis of luxe. Towels lose their absorbency and softness after years of washing.

“Avoid using too much detergent, which can build up on fibers,” Hayslett says. “Use a little vinegar during the rinse cycle to keep towels soft.” If your bath towels feel scratchy or start to smell musty despite washing, it’s time for a refresh.

To prolong the life of your towels, make sure you let them dry properly between uses to prevent mildew growth, noted Vyanca Soto of Market Studio Interiors. And you should definitely swap them out if you notice discoloration from your skincare products.

You likely spend plenty of time in your kitchen and bathroom each day. Make sure you take a few minutes to examine the grout lines around your bathtub and kitchen countertops.

“Grout breaks down over time, and you can end up with water damage to the drywall,” says Rebekah Zaveloff, cofounder and creative director at Imparfait Design Studio. “If you look and there are spots that have pulled away or deteriorated, it’s time to regrout.”

A sagging mattress is a clear sign it’s time for a new one. Mattresses lose support and can accumulate dust mites, allergens, and even mold, which is certainly not a dreamy way to slumber.

“Flip your mattress every six months [unless it's a one-sided design] to help maintain its structure and extend its lifespan,” Hayslette advised.

A mattress protector can extend its lifespan as does regular vacuuming to remove dust and debris. Soto recently bought a bed vacuum cleaner and says, “It works like a charm.” She also uses it on her dog’s bed to help it stay clean and smell fresher.

The lifespan of a pillow mainly depends on its material, whether that be foam, feathers, or another type of synthetic fill. But once a pillow of any kind loses its shape, it's time to say goodbye. Like mattresses, pillows can harbor dust mites, dead skin cells, and sweat over time. You can prolong a pillow's life with a pillow protector—Soto likes the Serta Power Chill—but you should still swap those out every year or so.

When to replace: two to three years for sheets; five to 10 years for duvets

Bed linen care and personal hygiene dictate the condition of your linens. Similar to pillows, sheets and duvets can accumulate dust mites, allergens, and bacteria, especially if you allow kids or pets into your bed.

“Over time, duvets lose their fluffiness and insulating properties due to regular use and washing,” says Rebecca Bobroff of Rebecca Bobroff Design.

If you’re prone to night sweats or use a lot of skin-care products, you may notice that your sheets become quickly discolored. Along with regular washing, you may want to stick to white sheets you can bleach over colors, which will look dingy even when they're clean.

“At a minimum, wash pillowcases once a week,” says Danielle Hurhula, head of home merchandising for Quince. “I’d recommend bumping washes up to twice a week if you suffer from allergies or asthma, sleep with hair or skincare products, or have sensitive or acne-prone skin.”

HVAC maintenance experts, including Energy Star, recommend checking filters monthly and replacing air filters every three months to help your system function properly and keep dust and allergens out of your home.

If you remove your filters and they look extra dirty, you may want to up the frequency. Doing so will not only improve indoor quality but keep your HVAC system from overworking and wasting energy, which could shave a few years off its life.

Water filters can accumulate bacteria, minerals, and other contaminants. This can affect the taste and quality of your drinking water.

“The lifespan of a filter cartridge is influenced by the level of contaminants and particulates in your water,” says Eric Roy, Ph.D., founder and chief scientist at Hydroviv. “Sink filter cartridges should be replaced approximately every six months and are designed to handle up to 720 gallons of water.”

You should also watch for a decrease in the flow rate, which indicates the filter is saturated.

When to replace: five years (or as recommended by the manufacturer)

You likely know that fridge water filters should be replaced every six months, but water lines can build up gunk or start leaking over time.

“Inspect the line for cracks or discoloration every year,” Soto suggests. “Flush it with vinegar and water every six months to prevent mineral buildup and ensure clean, clear water. My mom taught me this and when you see the color of the water when you flush it—you'll never stop doing it.”

Using sharp knives on wooden and plastic cutting boards can create grooves that embed bacteria, even after a dishwasher cycle. One study showed that plastic surfaces have the longest bacteria survival time, and scraping wooden boards with a knife significantly reduced bacterial presence.

Soto disinfects cutting boards with a vinegar solution and uses bleach for plastic boards. “Sand down wooden boards occasionally to remove deep grooves and re-oil them to maintain the wood,” she says.

Sponges harbor bacteria, especially when you use them to clean up raw food such as eggs and meat. In fact, salmonella can thrive on a sponge for more than seven days. Hayslette’s tip: Microwave damp sponges for one minute to sanitize between uses, but toss them after two weeks max.

Keep sponges as dry as possible, or better yet, replace them with dish brushes as these are less likely to breed bacteria, according to the International Journal of Food Microbiology. We like the Curio dish brush because it stands upright on a magnetic pedestal and you can replace the brush heads.

When to replace: three to five years (or sooner if scratched)

The nonstick coating on your cookware can wear off or become damaged over time. This decreases a pan's efficacy and also makes it a health hazard—the coating may start to flake off into your food.

“To extend their life, always use nonmetal utensils and hand-wash your pans instead of placing them in the dishwasher,” Hayslette says.

Not only do latex anti-slip rug pads lose cushioning over time, but they can also start to disintegrate.

“They get tacky and sticky on the floor, which can damage the floor's surface,” Zaveloff explains. Pull up your rug once in a while and if you see that the rug pads are turning dark yellow, start shopping for new ones.

They Want to Gene-Edit the World’s Deadliest Animal and Release It Into the Wild. What Happens Next Could Save Millions of Lives.

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

More pests might actually be the answer, researchers believe.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

Here’s what you’ll learn when you read this story:

It sounds like something out of science fiction: laboratories filled with thousands of genetically engineered animals kept in cages, all bred for the eventual purpose of being released into the wild. But that’s exactly what is happening at research institutions across Europe and North America where scientists are racing to create custom organisms—and save millions of lives.

Mosquitoes are the deadliest animal in the world. They kill around one million people every year by spreading malaria, dengue, yellow fever, and other deadly vector-borne diseases. Even as we try to protect ourselves with insect repellents, bed nets, and medications, they continue to be extremely effective at making us sick. Research suggests this problem will only get worse: rising temperatures and milder winters create the perfect conditions for the insects to increase their geographical range and active season. Meanwhile, the blood-suckers are developing resistance to many of our defenses.

In the face of this growing threat, experts are racing to find new ways to combat our small-but-mighty foe. At Target Malaria, an international research consortium headquartered in London working on genetic technologies to control malaria, scientists are using a groundbreaking gene-editing method to produce sterile female Anopheles gambiae mosquitoes, the primary vector for the transmission of human malaria. Diminishing the species’ population—especially in Africa, which accounts for 95 percent of malaria deaths, mostly in children under 5—could combat the deadly disease. However, some skeptics are concerned that this may open a door that can’t be shut.

To do this, researchers are using CRISPR-Cas9, which allows them to selectively modify DNA at precise locations in the genome, to induce something called a gene drive. This technology essentially bypasses the standard Mendelian system of inheritance—think back to your high school biology lecture on pea plants.

Like humans, mosquitoes have two copies of every gene. Some traits are dominant, which means they’re expressed even if an individual only has one copy of the gene. Others are recessive, which means both copies of the gene must be present for the gene to express itself. The gene drive skews the usual rules of inheritance so that a targeted trait—in this case, the gene related to infertility—is inherited at a higher rate. “If you disrupt only one copy of the gene, the mosquitoes are still fertile. But when you target both copies, the mosquitoes become sterile,” explains Alekos Simoni, PhD, a molecular biologist and technical support specialist at Target Malaria.

The thought is that, once researchers have enough mosquitoes with single copies of the gene drive, they’ll release the insects into the wild. Then, these mosquitoes will mate and pass on the mutation. This process will repeat with each generation, eventually causing a snowball effect.

“It’s a two-step process,” Simoni says. “So you first spread the gene drive, and when there are enough mosquitoes with the gene drive mating with each other, you produce sterile mosquitoes.” Eventually, “the population progressively declines,” he continues.

It seems like a straightforward plan in theory—but genetically engineering mosquitoes is a painstaking process. First, the researchers use a tiny needle to introduce the mutation in a mosquito embryo under a microscope. The scientists also include a fluorescent marker within the introduced genetic material that makes the modified mosquitoes glow in the dark, allowing them to easily track the insects. Then, they breed the modified mosquitoes and keep them in carefully controlled temperature, humidity, and light conditions.

“Then, we run a lot of different studies,” Simoni says. Researchers analyze how the gene drive transmits to the progeny, whether it impacts their behavior, and what the implications of releasing them into the wild might be.

Using sterilization to control insect populations is nothing new. A method called the Sterile Insect Technique (SIT) has been used since the 1950s to control populations of pests such as fruit flies and screwworm flies. In recent decades, it has been used in the United States to control Aedes aegypti mosquitoes, which can spread dengue, Zika, and chikungunya. SIT doesn’t involve any genetic engineering—it uses radiation to sterilize male mosquitoes that are then released into the wild. When these mosquitoes mate with a female, she will not produce any viable offspring.

Scaling SIT, however, is difficult. “You need to release 10 to 20 times as many males as the target population you want to control,” says Simoni, “and it also works very poorly in rural areas where there is very little infrastructure.” A gene drive, on the other hand, is based on releasing a relatively small number of mosquitoes, because it’s a process that spreads from generation to generation.

The latter method is still in its early stages—no gene-drive mosquitoes have been released anywhere yet. The first field trials will probably take place on islands or small areas, Simoni says, and will involve the release of mosquitoes on the order of thousands. Target Malaria previously had an approved release scheduled in the West African country of Burkina Faso, but the national authorities halted the effort.

Still, the work shows promise. In a 2024 study published in Nature Communications, researchers used a model to simulate the release of gene-drive mosquitoes in 16 areas across West Africa, each roughly 4,630 square miles in size. (For reference, Connecticut has a land area of roughly 4,842 square miles.) The team found that these releases could reduce the abundance of disease-carrying mosquitoes by 72 to 98 percent. Meanwhile, other unrelated teams are also using gene drives to engineer mosquitoes that are resistant to malaria parasites, which are what infect the insect in the first place.

Some skeptics have raised concerns over the potential use of gene-drive mosquitoes. After all, once you release a gene drive into the wild, you can’t easily reverse it. We don’t know how local ecosystems and food webs will respond to a decline in mosquito populations. Accidentally spreading organisms that carry the gene drive is also a major risk, according to a proposed code of ethics published in The CRISPR Journal. The technology also raises a more philosophical question: Is it morally acceptable for humans to potentially eradicate a species because it causes us harm?

Simoni thinks about this differently. He believes that it’s also an ethical concern to not intervene when there are tools available to solve a threat as deadly as malaria. “It’s always a matter of risks and benefits,” he says. When we’re thinking about a health problem that kills hundreds of thousands of people each year, “the ethical questions are: How can we stop it? If we have something that could potentially make an impact, why not use it?”

At the same time, researchers working on gene drives know that their work must be conducted responsibly. For instance, the team that wrote the aforementioned code of ethics recommends proactively incorporating ecological risk assessment into gene drive research. They also emphasize transparency, like conducting replicable studies and partnering with outside experts to mitigate any adverse effects of the work.

Simoni also stresses that gene-drive mosquitoes won’t be a silver bullet against vector-borne diseases. “A gene drive is something very promising,” Simoni says. “It’s a novel technology that has a lot of potential to make an impact, but not alone. It has to be used in combination with new vaccines, bed nets, or insecticides. But it can really make a difference.”

Would You Rather: Cadillac Escalade IQ vs. Escalade-V

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

For about $160,000, are you taking the 682-hp supercharged gas Escalade or the 750-hp electric one?

A lot of companies build EV models that have an internal-combustion analogue, with the guiding principle of, “the car you liked before, but electric.” There’s a tight thematic cohesiveness to that approach—a BMW i3 and M340i each endeavor to arrive at their optimized level of BMW 3-ness despite the differing powertrains—but the Cadillac Escalade is not like that. Yes, there are gas and electric versions, but they’re similar only inasmuch as they each avidly pursue the high-level Escalade goals of cosseting bigness and cocky braggadocio. When Tony Soprano got an Escalade in season 5 of The Sopranos, the only question around that casting decision was, what took so long? Today Tony would have more choices in his pursuit of the maximum expression of GM SUV, as do any of us with about $160,000 to spend. Namely, would you rather have a supercharged V-8 Escalade-V or an electric Escalade IQ?

Cadillac designed the Escalade-V’s exhaust to be extra boisterous, adding a 360-degree loop to the passenger’s-side pipe to equalize the length with the driver’s-side counterpart, for maximum sonic awesomeness. The IQ does not have equal-length dual exhaust. Whatever exhaust it does produce will get you into a tedious and possibly confrontational discussion of well-to-wheel emissions with your uncle at Thanksgiving. However, you’ll never have to worry about thieves stealing your catalytic converters, so I’ll call that one a draw.

See all results for used cars for sale near 80126

Looking at the specs, I’m surprised to see that the Escalade-V weighs eight pounds more than the IQ. Hold on, that doesn’t sound right—let me check my notes—sorry, my curb-weight figure for the Escalade-V inadvertently included a Toyota GR86 parked on its roof. Remove that extra car, and the supercharged Escalade is quite a bit lighter: 6290 pounds for the V, and a pavement-crushing 9120 pounds for the IQ.

Despite the weight disparity, the two Slades are evenly matched in a drag race, with the 750-hp IQ trailing the 682-hp V by a couple tenths of a second to 60 mph (4.5 seconds to 4.3). On a rolling start from 5 mph, they’re tied at 4.7 seconds, and passing a dawdler will be a quicker task in the IQ: 50 to 70 mph in 2.6 seconds, versus 3.1 seconds for the loud Caddy.

Now, I’m old enough to remember the days when a Ferrari 458 Italia felt like it had pretty decent passing power, but the Escalade IQ’s 2.1-second 30-to-50-mph time beats the Ferrari by a tenth. If the IQ had been part of our Chariots of the Gods comparison test in 2011, its 50-to-70-mph time would have slotted it a tenth of a second behind the 458 and two-tenths ahead of the McLaren MP4-12C. Neither of those, when accelerating, heave their hoods skyward like a 70-foot Viking sportfish climbing on a plane, but the Escalade IQ certainly does. And they say EVs don’t bring any drama to the experience—behind the wheel, the IQ accelerating like my favorite Ferrari of all time feels as improbable as it actually is, like the vehicle itself is struggling to cope with its superpowers. An Escalade-V drives more like a mutant CT5-V Blackwing, and it’s definitely the Escalade where the driver’s seat is more desirable than one of the chauffeur chairs in back.

See all results for used cars for sale near 80126

If we accept that one of the primary goals for an Escalade is to project brash confidence, and that hugeness is fundamental to that project, the Escalade IQ wins going away. It reminds me of a scaled-up Rolls-Royce Cullinan in the side profile, and there’s a lot of side to behold: the IQ is more than a foot longer than the gas-powered Escalade. It’s also just newer, and I think it helps the premium aura not to have a bunch of Chevys and GMCs out there copping your style with very similar bodies. Also: rear-axle steering is unequivocally a flex, and the internal-combustion Escalade doesn’t have it.

Inside, both Escalades offer huge screens, lots of leather, and power-operated doors. However, only the IQ is eligible for the Curated by Cadillac program, wherein buyers fork over an additional $54,995 and get to choose from a huge palette of colors, inside and out. With 44 interior color choices, you can certainly spec a one-of-one IQ, while the Escalade-V sticks with “Yukon Denali, but fancier” as a guiding principle. Advantage, IQ.

When we’re discussing EVs versus internal combustion, range is usually an easy win for the gas-powered candidate. But not here—the Escalade-V has an EPA-rated 312 miles of range, while the IQ gets a 465-mile rating. Yes, the V will refuel a lot quicker, but you’ll be stopping way more often. And if your gas pump stops at $100, you might technically need to fill twice to top off the 24-gallon tank with premium.

Another factor I have to consider is rap music references, and on that front the Escalade-V is the easy winner, if only because the IQ is too new—most Escalade lyrics concern gas-powered models by default. Cadillac did hire Big Boi from Outkast to rap about the IQ, but that doesn’t count. (“Escalade IQ, this is the intro to, the all-electric Cadillac perfection ...”) I’m sure we’ll get there eventually: “Rolling in the IQ to the house with a view, IQ got the roof see-through, park the IQ next to the other ’cause I just bought two.”

Finally, I think part of the Escalade mystique has always hinged on leadership. This is a boss truck, from Sopranos to Succession. And where are the luxury winds blowing? It seems to me that in this space, which is to say “not a V-12 Ferrari,” the market is amenable to electrification and the technology is progressing fast. GM just announced that its forthcoming battery chemistry will improve range while being 20 percent more compact. There will come a time, maybe not far off, when EVs just make more sense objectively (they already do, depending on use case) and internal combustion will be the nostalgia play. So in this Would You Rather, I’m embracing that future and going for the Escalade IQ. The V is charming, but for $160,000, the IQ better fulfills the role of flagship SUV—quick, cocky, luxurious, and incontrovertibly baller.

Ezra Dyer is a Car and Driver senior editor and columnist. He's now based in North Carolina but still remembers how to turn right. He owns a 2009 GEM e4 and once drove 206 mph. Those facts are mutually exclusive.
