Ocean Spray Just Gave Canned Cranberry Sauce A Chocolate Makeover

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Someone finally saw the potential in those jiggly, ridged slices.

Delish editors handpick every product we feature. We may earn commission from the links on this page.

Canned cranberry sauce has some pretty stiff competition at Thanksgiving. Between the mashed potatoes, stuffing, and mac and cheese, that jiggly little side dish often gets treated like the ugly stepsister of the dinner table. And it probably doesn't help that it comes out looking exactly like the can it was packaged in.

But perhaps a little chocolate could improve its reputation.

Ocean Spray and Los Angeles chocolatier Compartés have teamed up with recipe developer Carolina Gelen on a new treat called Chocolate Covered Cranberry Sauce, taking the familiar Ocean Spray stuff and turning it into chocolate-coated discs, sold in packs of four.

If that sounds vaguely familiar, you may have seen Gelen's original Instagram video from 2023, when she experimented with dipping slices of canned cranberry sauce in chocolate (the recipe has since racked up more than 10 million views).

Ocean Spray's Kelvin Vuong said Gelen's recipe was exactly the kind of unexpected use for cranberry sauce the company wanted to share with more people. The brand eventually partnered with her and Compartés to turn the homemade recipe into something people could purchase—and Gelen is pretty thrilled to see her original experiment come this far.

"Getting to work with Ocean Spray and Compartés to turn that idea into something people across the country can try is incredibly exciting," she said in the announcement.

As for why this combination works, Compartés' creative director and chief chocolatier, Jonathan Grahm, pointed to the contrast between the cranberries' sharp, sour flavor and the richness of the chocolate. It's the same reason tart fruit and chocolate can work so well together, even if cranberry sauce isn't exactly the first thing you'd think to dip.

The limited-edition chocolates are available exclusively at Compartés for $24.95 per four-pack, while supplies last, with the gourmet chocolate brand also making chocolate turkeys filled with Ocean Spray's whole-berry cranberry sauce in both milk and dark chocolate options.

Cranberry sauce might never get the same love as mashed potatoes, but chocolate certainly gives it a fighting chance.

Poppi founders are going after PepsiCo's Gatorade with new beverage startup Jetty

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

After disrupting the soda category with Poppi, founders Allison and Stephen Ellsworth are now taking aim at hydration with their new brand, Jetty.

Starting Monday, Jetty drinks and ready-to-mix electrolyte packets will be on shelves at Whole Foods grocery stores and available online at Target and Amazon.

The new, self-funded venture directly pits the Ellsworths against PepsiCo, which bought Poppi for $1.95 billion in 2025. Pepsi owns Gatorade, which dominates the U.S. sports drink category with about 60% market share, according to Numerator data.

Poppi shook up the soda category with prebiotic fiber, low calorie and sugar counts and its fun marketing aimed at Generation Z and millennials. Jetty looks to upend the hydration category through a similar strategy. To do so, it will face off not only against Gatorade but also a swarm of other upstarts looking to get in on the electrolyte-fueled trend.

For decades, Gatorade and other sports drinks have pitched their products to athletes who need electrolytes and fluids after an intense workout, although many consumers drink them every day without even sweating. Jetty is aimed at people who need a hydration boost, but not the same level of electrolytes or sugar as a traditional sports drink.

"No one has really created a product for just everyday hydration — literally everyday hydration for normal people," Allison Ellsworth, Jetty's chief brand officer, told CNBC. "Like I woke up this morning, I didn't run a marathon."

Following Poppi's sale to PepsiCo, Ellsworth stayed on as an advisor to the brand, although she said she is no longer involved in the day-to-day operations.

After 10 years building Poppi, Ellsworth said, she finally had free time. As Ellsworth tells it, she went to the doctor for the first time in a decade. She started using a GLP-1 medication and lost 60 pounds.

Ellsworth's husband, Stephen, now CEO of the new brand, noticed she wasn't hydrating, even when she came back from the gym.

GLP-1 medications such as Ozempic and Mounjaro can make people less thirsty. But because staying hydrated can also help mitigate some of the drugs' side effects, such as nausea and headaches, it can become more important.

"I physically struggle getting liquid into my body," Ellsworth said.

Plain old water is often less appealing to consumers, even dehydrated GLP-1 users, but more flavorful options with added benefits can be tempting. About 44% of GLP-1 users report consuming more functional beverages, according to EY-Parthenon's third annual survey of more than 3,600 current or former GLP-1 users.

Ellsworth sees a broader appeal for Jetty, too.

"Our core audience is going to be millennial moms and Gen Z," she said. "Gen Z just isn't relating to these old school brands, the legacy of the Gatorades and the Powerades. They're just not cool."

Consumers broadly have become more interested in staying hydrated.

Dollar sales of hydration and electrolyte products climbed 20% to $2.6 billion over the 52 weeks ended Oct. 4, based on data from SPINS, which tracks new universal product codes, or UPCs. The category also has a strong repeat buyer rate of 62%.

However, the Ellsworths aren't the only ones to notice the potential in hydration. The number of new items in the category with annual sales of at least $10,000 has increased 41% over the last two years, according to SPINS.

Big food and beverage companies have been looking to hydration, too.

Kraft Heinz debuted Capri Sun juice pouches and Kool-Aid packets with electrolytes this spring. In April, Pepsi unveiled an overhaul of the Gatorade brand, focused on making it more appealing to a wider swath of consumers, with more potential occasions like a "fresh morning start" or an "afternoon walk." And Electrolit is a top performer for its distributor Keurig Dr Pepper, with 40% retail sales growth in the company's third quarter.

All of that means more competition for shelf space and consumers' "share of throat," an industry term for beverage market share.

To increase Jetty's visibility across the grocery store and to maximize convenience for shoppers, Jetty will be available in both powders and bottles.

Single-use electrolyte mixes are growing at a much faster clip than the ready-to-drink category, Ellsworth said, thanks to Unilever's Liquid I.V. and smaller upstarts like LMNT. On Amazon alone, powders are a $1 billion category, according to Ellsworth.

The ready-to-drink, or RTD, sports beverages are a much larger market, but the category's sales are shrinking as consumers reach for other hydration options instead. Unit sales of sports drinks have fallen 9.1% from 2022 to 2026, according to Numerator data. And Coca-Cola has written down the value of BodyArmor by more than $1.7 billion after buying control of the brand for $5.6 billion in late 2021.

"Me and my husband know how to do RTD," Ellsworth said.

Jetty will launch with five flavors, including watermelon, orange mango, fruit punch, black raspberry and white cherry. All of them contain 100 milligrams of sodium, 310 milligrams of potassium and 100 milligrams of magnesium in a 15.2-ounce bottle or 15-gram packet.

In addition to electrolytes, Jetty products also contain B vitamins, 3 grams of fiber and 6 grams of sugar. It doesn't use artificial sweeteners or dyes, its founders say.

For comparison, a 20-ounce bottle of traditional Gatorade Thirst Quencher has about 270 milligrams of sodium and 80 milligrams of potassium, plus 34 grams of sugar. The sports drink brand is also phasing out artificial colors and promoting lower-sugar versions.

"What's really interesting about this product and this opportunity is we now can capture all those lapsed drinkers — or that generation that didn't even ever drink it in the first place," Ellsworth said.

Poppi's new owner knows about Jetty and the market it is trying to capture, too.

"I don't think [Pepsi] gets it, but what they do get is that the movement is changing, and they think that just by taking dyes out or doing 90% less sugar, they're going to capture this audience," Ellsworth said. "But that is very big corporate thinking."

She added that she believes Pepsi has missed the mark on advertising to Gen Z consumers across its portfolio, such as in its campaigns for Sprite competitor Starry.

Perhaps as an extension of that, Poppi's performance has been underwhelming under PepsiCo's ownership.

The food and beverage giant now estimates the fair value of potential performance-based payments to Poppi's prior owners is $12 million, Pepsi said in a regulatory filing in October. At the time of the deal, Pepsi promised an additional payment of $300 million if Poppi hit certain targets by the third quarter of 2027. The Ellsworths declined to comment on the filing to CNBC.

This time around, with one successful brand under their belts, Jetty's founders say they have learned from past mistakes. Those lessons should help Jetty as it launches at a much larger scale than Poppi did.

Three different claims lawyers have signed off on Jetty's packaging, according to Ellsworth. Poppi paid $8.9 million to settle class-action litigation alleging false advertising over its "gut healthy" claims.

For now, Jetty is entirely self-funded with $2.5 million, although Ellsworth said that the brand will likely raise money in 2027. This time, though, she said investors will probably look less like traditional venture capitalists.

"I want to bring in investors that bring value," Ellsworth said. Instead of a venture capitalist who is willing to invest $15 million, Jetty would rather consider investors who can only write a check for $1 million but have access to celebrities. "So then you're getting access to talent on a cheaper level and people that are invested in the brand."

And Jetty has also learned a few lessons from Gatorade. For example, the brand is already at work on a lemon-lime version — the original flavor of Gatorade.

"We'll eventually do it," Ellsworth said. "But it was really important at the beginning just to stick to flavors that we love, for it to taste good."

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

EDGE Magazine speaks to McMillen on the creation of his latest hit

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Everything began with an unloved game jam prompt. In August 2012, fresh off The Binding of Isaac's enormous success, Edmund McMillen took part in Ludum Dare 24. The theme for that competition's entries was put to the vote until, after five rounds, the organisers were left with a top 20. "Plus one joke theme," according to the now-deprecated webpage: "1,000 kittens."

This theme came dead last in public voting. McMillen took it as his inspiration regardless, and together with Team Meat co-founder Tommy Refenes began prototyping a game that featured not just 1,000 cats but – according to an early marketing tagline – 12 sextillion of them. "I just love the idea of going infinite," McMillen says. This number was achieved using a method McMillen had developed with his previous game, using Flash to add multiple layers of ‘animation clips' to augment the character's look. "I really liked how he'd change visually as you went," he says of Isaac. "And that was the initial spark. I could make a cat-like shape with a bunch of different clips for arms, legs, bodies, heads, whatever – randomise them. We could simulate those as DNA, and then you could have the cats breed and spit out all these different combinations."

It was a simple idea, but one McMillen found himself "pretty obsessed with". Dig up his blog posts from the time, and the enthusiasm is evident. "It's been very hard to keep this one a secret," he wrote in the announcement post for Mew-Genics, as it was then known. This was in October 2012, just weeks after the game jam.

Of course, the game would go on to be a success that dwarfed even Binding of Isaac, selling a million copies in its first week of release. But it'd take an awfully long time to get there, and at the beginning, in the case of everyone but McMillen, the idea was as unloved as the prompt that had spawned it.

A year on from that fateful Ludum Dare, McMillen and Refenes had a version of the game they reckoned they were ready to show in public – albeit not one that looked anything like the Mewgenics we know today. "There was a house you could upgrade over time, where you could interact with the cats, and they'd have these wacky animations, and then use those cats as avatars in minigames," McMillen recalls. "I think we only had one functional minigame, the cat fights, which was this Pokémon-esque turn-based combat game."

That was the version of the game Team Meat intended to demo at PAX Seattle in August 2013. At the very last minute, there was a change of mind, and the pair attended without the playable build they'd announced. It proved to be a turning point in more ways than one.

"When we came back from PAX, Tommy was like, ‘I can't work on this anymore," McMillen says. "And I begged, I pleaded… but it was cancelled. That was it. There was nothing I could really do about it." At the time, Refenes' sole interest was making a follow-up to Team Meat's hit platformer, which would be announced the following August as Super Meat Boy Forever. In the announcement blogpost, McMillen explained: "Mewgenics is not cancelled, we just put it on hold to work full time on this one."

Sign up to our Switch 2 Spotlight newsletter to make sure you get the very latest news on Nintendo's new console and its games every week

Super Meat Boy Forever would eventually be released in 2020. By then, McMillen was no longer part of the project, or Team Meat. "I realised we wanted very different things," he reflects now. At the time, though, he kept trying to make the Mewgenics dream a reality. "I was still pitching it to Tommy before I left. I was trying to wrap it in a genre that I thought he would be excited about."

One version McMillen remembers was closer to Spelunky, the game that had inspired his step into Roguelikes with Isaac. Another came from his longtime friend and collaborator Tyler Glaiel, a fellow traveller in the '00s Flash webgame scene. "I remember suggesting back then: ‘Why not try Final Fantasy Tactics-style combat instead of Pokémon combat?'" Glaiel says. "Because they're on the same tree, kind of, but you can do a lot more with the positioning stuff." McMillen laughs: "Yeah. One has depth, and the other has the illusion of depth."

After McMillen left Team Meat in December 2016, he and Glaiel were free to work together again – not on Mewgenics, but on action platformer The End Is Nigh. "The whole time this was happening, I kept pushing these Mewgenics-like ideas at Tyler," McMillen says. One was a game featuring randomly generated aliens that the player could breed.

"There were some differences," Glaiel says. "The animation was weirder, because it was more rigged and physics-y, because the body parts were so different from each other." Still, Glaiel knew exactly what his old friend was up to. "You just wanted to make Mewgenics, I could tell," he says to McMillen. And, after working on the alien prototype for a week or so, he concluded: "This is a worse theme than cats." After all, it lacked the heartfelt motivations that had driven McMillen to this idea in the first place.

Because, despite the way Mewgenics encourages you to treat your cats, the developer is an animal lover. "Our first cat, Guppy, was a stray kitten that was abandoned in a planter box by its mother," he says. Guppy passed away in 2014 but was immortalised as a powerful character transformation in The Binding of Isaac. "And then, with money and whatever else, my wife was like, ‘I want a hairless cat.'"

That was his introduction to the world of designer cat breeding. The McMillen family have owned three hairless cats, the latest "a Russian import named Puggy", but he has little love for the industry that produces them, which he feels prioritises aesthetic features – unusual ear shapes or fur patterns – over the health of the cats themselves. "They're breeding for rare drops! It's the same as in the game!" These words are delivered with a righteous fury that seems to have burned in McMillen through the long journey from inception to release. "And that's what really kept me going throughout."

After The End is Nigh shipped in 2017, McMillen and Glaiel were finally ready to start turning this idea into a reality. The game's themes were already in place, as was a visual style and a cast of characters: Dr Beanies, Butch and the rest made their debut in blogposts dating back to 2012. You can even find an early version of the theme tune, recorded by rock duo Ridiculon. The one remaining question: what kind of game was this going to be?

"There's a bunch of prototypes from that era," Glaiel says. One was "a Castle Crashers-esque beat-'em-up". That proved difficult given the developers wanted multiple characters in play at once, "because you can tell more interesting stories when it's a group, instead of just one cat". So how were they to make that work in real time, without taking the multiplayer route? Glaiel remembers some possible solutions: "What if you have one main cat and a bunch of smaller follower cats that repeat what you do? What if it's just three AI-controlled cats, and one main cat you're controlling?"

They also experimented with making a StarCraft-style RTS. That proved to be "a nightmare to control," Glaiel says. "There was no strategy. You couldn't dodge attacks because you didn't have direct control. You could only tell things what to do, so if an enemy was going to hit you, it had to have a five-second windup so you had time to click your cats and move them away."

"It's funny," McMillen says to his friend. "When you describe them now, they don't sound bad." Glaiel agrees: "Both of those prototypes, I think there's a world where we executed on them and made a decent game. Probably not as interesting as the one we ended up making…"

"Both of those prototypes, I think there's a world where we executed on them and made a decent game..."

The game they ended up making, of course, came back to Glaiel's old suggestion of turn-based tactics. Years earlier, McMillen had brushed off the idea because it would mean an awful lot of work. "And that's true," says Glaiel, having worked on this incarnation of the game from 2020 to 2026. "Certainly can't disagree with that!"

Part of what made things so complicated was that the game had two layers. The pet sim McMillen had started out with, and the tactical RPG Glaiel had introduced to the mix were dependent on each other to function. The specifics of the cat-breeding system couldn't be nailed down until they'd "made sure the combat was working fine," Glaiel explains. "Because the breeding doesn't really matter if you don't have anything to use the cats you're breeding for."

Their solution to this sticky situation? "We just kind of worked on the systems seemingly at random, until they were mostly complete, and then we connected them all together," McMillen says. "And we never spoke about it, really." This was only possible because of the pair's dynamic and shared sensibilities – and even so, it didn't always work out. "We were just kind of hoping our assumptions were correct," McMillen says. "And some of them were wrong."

One of those dead ends involved the game's cast of weirdo NPCs, who'd been around since 2012. What role they'd actually serve in-game didn't become clear until late in development, McMillen says. "Initially, the idea was that you'd upgrade the NPCs with money." They didn't like the player grind that encouraged, and so he searched for an alternative before eventually realising: "Wait, we have a resource right here. We have cats."

In the final game, any kittens with undesirable traits or elderly fighters past their prime can be dumped on these characters in exchange for rewards – something else that wasn't obvious at first. "Dr Beanies giving you side quests, we figured that out less than a year before the game came out," Glaiel says. "Before that, he'd give you an ability needle, with a random ability from one of the cats you'd sent him. And they'd just clog up your inventory, because most of the time it would just be some colourless ability."

Rather than ditching this idea entirely, however, the developers found another home for it – you can obtain ability needles by beating an optional hard route with one of the late-game areas. "There's a lot of that," McMillen says, "where we'll do a system, it'll be really in-depth and interesting, but maybe doesn't work how we intended. We don't forget it's there. We make a mental note, and in the future, when there's a hole we need to fill, we can just pull that shit back out."

This resulted in one of Mewgenics' greatest strengths: its wealth of ideas, where something originally designed as a headline feature might be tucked away where relatively few players will ever find it. "The people who have been playing it hardcore since release are starting to go, ‘I've got 300 hours in this game, and I've never seen this before'," McMillen says. "There's a tremendous amount of this very rare, difficult-to-find shit that we just kept adding. And those details, even though they're somewhat needless at times, create the illusion this is a living world. That's where the magic is with games like this."

That's something McMillen learned with Isaac, over the many post-launch updates developed alongside Mewgenics' very long journey to release. He admits to feeling competitive with his own game: "The bar got raised so high, especially with where Isaac ended. Going into this [project], we had this not-so-indirect comparison of: ‘This is what we're going up against – this game that has ten years of development content in it already'."

Although it's less than a year old, Mewgenics already feels like a game that's undergone a similar decade-long period of iteration and expansion. And with many millions of cats having already been fed through its meat grinder, McMillen and Glaiel have been able to see that old magic at work over and over again.

"There are things we forgot were in the game," Glaiel says. He describes an online discussion where a player was trying to figure out why a particular unit had been blinded in battle. "And it's like, ‘Oh, he was standing on a dirt tile, and he used a wind ability – and that apparently blinds them'. I don't remember putting that interaction in the game."

This is apparently news to McMillen, too: "I remember we briefly joked about it very early on, when we made those tiles. I didn't know it was ever implemented." The pair begin trading possible interactions, wondering if they're in the game or not, or whether they should be. Do holy-element powers cause grass to grow, the same way water does? Gravity powers crush grass, and immobilise flying units – but what about water? And how does it interact with rocks?

"I don't think it does a lot," Glaiel replies. "It's mostly just there as a tag for Psychics to interact with." And that gets them onto the ways the elements' effects could be expanded in future. Glaiel, on the spot, starts musing: "One of our DLC classes could probably do stuff with gravity…" When it comes to "going infinite", McMillen has clearly met his match.

Android tablets are back, but are they for you?

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Android tablets have always been a bit of a touchy subject, but it’s abundantly clear that the form factor is making a comeback. But, as someone who’s a fan of the smaller sizes, are you actually using the gargantuan Android tablets that every company seems obsessed with? In other words – are you the customer for the Android tablets these brands are selling?

9to5Google Weekender by Ben Schoon is a part of our free newsletter that also includes ‘Inbox,’ a twice-weekly roundup of key stories from around the web by Daniel Bader. Sign up here to get Weekender delivered to your inbox every Sunday along with ‘Inbox’ on Tuesday and Thursday. You can find past issues here.

For a while there, Android tablets had hit what felt like a dead end. Bigger smartphones and cooling of the tablet market as a whole, along with the general sentiment that Apple was doing a way better job with the iPad anyway, left the category in a place that felt stagnant – and that’s the nice way of putting it.

But, over the past few years, Android tablets have had a renaissance.

New form factors, big releases from a variety of brands, and flagship-tier devices left and right. Despite Android’s push into foldables and still-huge displays on nearly every smartphone, tablets are very much back. Prior to RAMageddon’s worst impacts, tablet sales were growing for nearly everyone, and Android brands were seeing a larger slice of the pie.

Anyway, this is on my mind right now because I’ve had a lot of Android tablets arrive in my office in the past few weeks, capping off a lot of tablet announcements in the past few months. I just finished up with Redmagic Astra 2, a tablet I think fits the description of “Android’s iPad Mini OLED,” and I’m in the process of moving into tablets from Lenovo and Amazon. Our Jordan Floyd has also been testing out the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra behind the scenes. And if there’s one trend amongst recent tablet releases, it’s that big tablets seem to be the primary focus among a lot of brands.

Take Amazon’s new Google Play-equipped Alexa Tablet lineup, for example.

The Alexa Tablet 12 is the crown jewel of the lineup, packing the most powerful specs and a premium anti-glare display. It’s the only one that gets a “Pro” moniker, while the 11-inch and 8-inch models are noticeable steps down. Unboxing the Alexa Tablet 8 this week, for example, I was immediately reminded this is not a premium device – the screen makes that clear without any hesitation whatsoever. It was a case of whiplash after setting up the Lenovo Yoga Tab Plus Gen 2 with its 4K display.

It’s a weird one for me. I’ve never been a big fan of these super-sized Android tablets (or iPads, for that matter). I can get behind a 10-inch or 11-inch model, but I find myself more at home on the smaller slates. They’re fantastic for travel as an alternative to draining my phone’s battery, while I find myself just reaching for a laptop instead if I want a bigger screen. A big tablet, regardless of specs, can rarely replace a proper laptop for me, so lugging both around just sounds like more trouble than it’s worth.

But, clearly, people are buying these bigger slates. Samsung appears to be seeing enough success with its mammoth Galaxy Tab S Ultra lineup to keep the 14.6-inch tablet around each year while the smaller sizes are now on an every-other-year cycle. Lenovo’s lineup is virtually all underpowered when the screen size goes down, aside from the Legion Tab that’s now $900.

Are you buying these bigger tablets? What are you using them for?

I imagine that most folks opting for a bigger tablet are using these in the place of a laptop. Thinner and lighter, longer battery life, etc. And in cases like the Alexa Tablet 12 Pro and its $550~ price tag, I get the appeal. Lenovo Yoga Tab Plus Gen 2 takes that down a notch for me at $799 – you’re getting into laptop territory there – but it still is a compelling-enough alternative if it fits your needs. The Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra at $1,400 plus a $200 keyboard, yeah I don’t get that one at all.

Let’s discuss! I want to know if you’re using an Android tablet, what you’re using it for, and what size you actually prefer.

Ladies and gents, we’ve finally got a new flagship in the US. Motorola Signature 27 is launching on November 19 and looks excellent, though its flashy spec sheet won’t come cheap at $1,599.

Finally, something good came out of Googlebook

Googlebook is a disaster, but there’s finally one good thing coming out of it in Henry Cavill’s new “Mustache Protocol” game which, supposedly, was built on Googlebook. I’ll bite my tongue there, but the accompanying video brought a needed bit of joy into my week.

Nov. 8, 1656: The birth of Edmond Halley

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

It’s been said that the general public knows three astronomical objects: the Moon, the rings of Saturn, and Halley’s Comet. English astronomer Edmond Halley, born Nov. 8, 1656, didn’t discover the object named for him. In fact, he never even saw it — but his mathematical prediction of its return in 1758 guaranteed his place in history.

Halley was a friend of Isaac Newton and used Newton’s theory of gravity and to calculate “his” comet’s orbit. In fact, Halley published at his own expense the book Newton wrote explaining the theory, Philosophiæ Naturalis Principia Mathematica. In 1676, Halley sailed to the island of St. Helena in the South Atlantic Ocean and spent a year measuring stellar positions, the result of which was the first catalog of the southern sky.

Michael E. Bakich is a contributing editor for Astronomy magazine. With degrees in astronomy and planetarium education, he has led many eclipse tours, published several books on observing, and logged thousands of hours at the eyepiece.

Feature: Five Reasons Why 2026 Is The Best Time Ever To Be An Xbox Series X|S Owner

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

The two consoles are celebrating their sixth anniversary on November 10th

We've got a lot of anniversaries taking place over the next few weeks, including the big 25th anniversary of Xbox on November 15th, but we thought we'd first take a moment to celebrate the Xbox Series X|S anniversary as well.

Both of these consoles launched six years ago on November 10th of 2020, and Pure Xbox was there at the time with a big celebration of the launch along with an unboxing preview, a full review, and various bits of coverage.

Hardware Review Xbox Series X - The Fastest, Most Powerful Xbox Ever

A lot has changed over this past six years for Xbox (and indeed, the gaming industry at large), and not all of it has been for the better by any means. As someone who picked up an Xbox Series X at launch though, I reckon 2026 has been the best time ever to own the console, so this seems like a good chance to highlight some reasons why!

Here are those five reasons — in my personal opinion — on why it's so good to own an Xbox Series console right now:

Prior to this year, there had been a lot of disgruntlement amongst the fanbase over the "This is an Xbox" marketing campaign that essentially told us the Xbox Series X and Xbox Series S weren't all that important anymore. That might not have been the intention, but it's certainly the impression that many fans came away with.

We've seen a complete change in 2026 though, with Asha Sharma's new leadership team insisting that console is a major focus for them, and implementing an absolute ton of new console-specific features throughout the year. From Mythic Achievements to new startup animations, it's been an amazing year for console updates without a doubt.

The obvious benefit to being an Xbox Series X|S owner this late in the lifecycle is that you get access to a tonne of great games from over the years. 2026 has been an incredible year for new releases too — both first-party and third-party — and I think Xbox Game Pass also enjoyed another really fruitful year complete with many "day one" releases.

My personal backlog is extensive at this point, and it doesn't help that ID@Xbox keeps knocking it out of the park with so many great indie releases on a monthly basis. Xbox Play Anywhere is growing on an extremely impressive scale as well, meaning I can play loads of my purchased Series X|S games on PC whenever I want to.

Despite what some might think, we've had exclusives on Xbox consoles over the past six years, but most of them have been of the third-party variety. Gears of War: E-Day changed all that in October when it become the first permanent console exclusive that Xbox has published in a long time, and it'll be followed up by Clockwork Revolution in 2027.

In other words, you're going to need an Xbox Series X, an Xbox Series S or a powerful enough PC to play these games going forward, which certainly makes these consoles more attractive for fans of certain titles and franchises. I'm certainly comforted knowing that I already own a Series X for when Xbox decides to make even more games exclusive.

Grand Theft Auto 6 is going to have the biggest video game launch of all time, and that's only going to happen across PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X|S. There's no PC version whatsoever right now, and fans could be waiting until well into next year until they get a native Windows release of the game.

That automatically makes it a fantastic time to be an Xbox Series X|S owner if you're interested in GTA 6! All of the pre-release footage has been on base PS5, which means it should look basically the same on the Xbox Series X. Everyone's a little bit more concerned about the Series S version, but this is Rockstar we're talking about - they're surely going to pull out all the stops to optimise the biggest game ever for Microsoft's lower-powered console.

It's not usually the case that we're talking about a strong future for six-year-old consoles, but the video game industry isn't in a normal place right now. Everyone knows about the component crisis that's causing prices to vastly increase across the board - something that's affected new purchases of the Series X and S as well.

However, with rumours that Xbox's Project Helix console (potentially set for 2028) could cost well over $1,000, it seems more likely than ever that the Xbox Series X and Xbox Series S have still got long futures ahead of them. Combine that with all the new console features that have been added this year, plus the arrival of new technology such as AMD FSR Upscaling 4.1, and it looks like there's a lot more life left in these consoles going forward.

Again, I appreciate that it's not a great time to buy an Xbox Series X or Series S right now due to the aforementioned component crisis, and here's hoping we might at least get some Black Friday deals in a few weeks' time.

For the rest of us who already own one though, this has got to be the best time ever, right? Amazing games, loads of new features, exclusives, GTA 6... I'm very glad to own an Xbox Series X sitting here in November of 2026.

My only fear? That it'll suddenly break and I'll have spend an absolute fortune on a new one.

@TheFakulty That's not it. Keep in mind that Switch, as a platform, is simply a device, or rather, two devices. Xbox is no longer a closed device, the platform is more of an ecosystem that extends across countless devices.

What does this mean? It means you have your profile, library, achievements, and saves… free from the limitations of a single device, and that in turn adds value to each device individually, you’re not held back by the limitations of a single device.

To me, this is truly incredible. In my case, I prioritize hardware power, so the Xbox PC takes center stage for me (I wish there were a Nintendo PC so I could play Zelda much better), but everyone has their own preferences and may even change them over time: You might go through a phase of playing on a handheld, buy a PC with the latest GPU and enjoy it to the fullest, or go through a period without dedicated hardware and play directly on your smart TV or even your smartphone. Xbox makes all of this possible with a single profile, and that’s a huge benefit, at least for me

Not just the profile, achievements and saves, but also your purchase history is respected on Xbox. This is why Play Anywhere is better than Nintendo and Sony's alternatives. Continuing with The Legend of Zelda Breath of the Wild example, you need to pay an upgrade fee for extra frames and resolution on Switch 2 after buying the Switch version. Sony's PC ports are separate purchases, and the console upgrades aren't free. Moreover, you cannot play Nintendo's games on PC at all (officially), so you're stuck on Nintendo's relatively weak hardware. The differences are significant. Play Anywhere is on another level.

I have seen a lot of good improvements on the console side this year, adding great features like free cloud saves and quick resume. The brand resonance feels much better than at the end of 2025.

There is, of course, much more work to do, but it feels like the new leadership teams across operations, development, marketing, and technology are moving in a positive direction. The studios that were a resource drain are being closed/sold off, and there is greater clarity on what Xbox wants to do in the future.

I am also glad to see some exclusives coming back, and Xbox Play Anywhere is a great feature for those who want to play across console, handheld, and PC with one copy of a game with cloud saves that follow you. It is more open than the closed ecosystems of my Switch 2 and PS5, which offer portability, but not flexibility across devices.

Of course, there will be some who just dislike Xbox and will never be happy with anything other than the closure of Microsoft. They are best ignored.

Opinion: Onimusha vs Phantom Blade Zero - The Insane Sword Fight for Game of the Year

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Onimusha: Way of the Sword and Phantom Blade Zero are two of the best action games I've played in years.

I've always been a sucker for cool sword fights and I'd argue that these two games do sword fights better than almost any other (Ghost of Tsushima and Ghost of Yotei deserve a big shoutout, mind).

I genuinely feel like I've hit the jackpot, having both of these blade-wielding masterpieces drop within the space of two months.

And I've got very little doubt: it's going to be Onimusha and Phantom Blade Zero vying for my eventual Game of the Year vote. And at the time of writing this article, I've got no idea which way I'm going to swing.

To be clear, I know Grand Theft Auto 6 is just around the corner — and yes, it could absolutely throw a spanner in the works — but taking my own personal taste into account, it would have to be properly mind-blowing to top the two titles I've already mentioned.

So I wanted to try and take a step back, and re-examine both Onimusha and Phantom Blade Zero. Break them down a bit and at least attempt to decide on which one I like better.

Admittedly, we might not get to that point today, but it should still make for a good discussion on what actually makes a great action game.

Believe it or not, enjoyable action is crucial to making a top tier action title — but picking a winner in this category isn't going to be easy.

That's mainly because the two games operate so differently. While they share a focus on defensive techniques that subsequently open your opponents up to retaliation, the overall feel is completely different.

Onimusha is very measured and, for lack of a better word, slow. Comparatively, Phantom Blade Zero is lightning quick. Their rhythms couldn't be much more distinct from one another.

Interestingly, though, both games prioritise cinematic flair — but critically, they do it without sacrificing responsive controls. I think this is a huge factor in why they stand apart from many of their peers; both games are a masterclass in providing eye-popping but very readable action.

Although I will say, Phantom Blade Zero pushes the limits of that last point at times. Where Onimusha affords itself clarity through its more methodical clashing of swords, Zero can appear a little too busy during its more frantic battles.

And I think this translates, very subtly, into Phantom Blade Zero feeling more unwieldy. You press a button once in Onimusha and Musashi swings his sword once. You do the same in Zero and Soul might slash three times depending on the weapon he has equipped.

That's part of Zero's wuxia identity, of course, but the seemingly never-ending combos can sometimes come off as being flashy for the sake of flashiness. In contrast, every strike in Capcom's offering feels like it has a purpose and a weight to it.

So suddenly, I find myself leaning towards Onimusha here... but then Zero takes the cake with regards to variety.

I absolutely love the weapon system in S-GAME's debut. There are 24 of them to find throughout the game, and they're all unique, with their own movesets and accompanying perks. Coming across a new blade is genuinely exciting, because you know the developer's put serious time and effort into designing them.

Onimusha just can't compete — but that's also by design. Capcom clearly wanted to flesh out Musashi's single sword to an obsessive extent, and that's exactly what it did. He's got one primary weapon, and you learn to master it over the course of his adventure.

And this is reflected in the sheer number of unique, contextual animations found in Onimusha. You can't really delve into that level of detail if you're animating for 24 weapons — although S-GAME certainly tries, with each blade boasting its own finishers and standard combos.

It's a real 50-50, then; the action is absolutely brilliant in both games, but for different reasons. There's part of me, though, that just adores Capcom's commitment to weight. Whether it's Monster Hunter, Devil May Cry, Dragon's Dogma, or Onimusha, no one does satisfyingly crunchy combat like the Japanese company.

And so purely in terms of feel, I think Onimusha sneaks the win. It's a more refined experience overall.

Bosses play a gigantic role in this debate. They're the highlights of both games, punctuating pivotal story moments, providing sheer spectacle, and putting your skills to the test.

Numbers-wise, Phantom Blade Zero wins this one hands down. The game's got a really, really impressive lineup of unique boss battles; outside of several big bad guys that share select attack animations, there are no repeats to be found.

I honestly can't think of another action title that can equal its roster.

You could argue, however, that Onimusha's more focused approach makes each boss feel more memorable. I do think Zero's myriad opponents blend together a touch; I'd struggle to recall a lot of them if you were to put me on the spot.

But Onimusha also has a repeat problem. It reuses certain boss encounters multiple times, and even though the act of clashing with them is always engaging because of how strong the combat system is, it does get to a point later on where you find yourself asking "this guy again?".

The bottom line here is that both games have a range of incredible boss encounters — Onimusha in particular's got a few of my all-time favourites, like the brothers — but ultimately, it's so hard to look past the variety that's on display in Phantom Blade Zero.

When it comes to story and characters, I don't think there's any competition.

Phantom Blade Zero's narrative is arguably its weakest aspect. I think the plot itself is quite interesting once events start falling into place in the game's latter half, but until then, it's a mishmash of often incoherent flashbacks and exposition-heavy dialogue.

The story's sloppily told, and there are way too many notes and memory flowers — basically audio logs — to find in order to understand the full context of what's going on.

By contrast, Onimusha tells a story that's smaller in overall scope, with a greater emphasis on character interactions. The narrative stuff never outstays its welcome, with snappy dialogue and frankly fantastic cutscenes.

Plus, Musashi is a deeply endearing protagonist.

Phantom Blade Zero's got some amazing cinematics as well — the animated ones are superb — but they can't have the same impact when the context surrounding them is often so murky.

Placing great gameplay within a world that's lovely to look at and a joy to explore can be what truly elevates an action game.

The recent Ninja Gaiden 4, for example, is an absolute blast to play — but its drab, samey environments prevent it from feeling like a memorable journey.

Phantom Blade Zero certainly doesn't have that problem. Each chapter takes place within a visually distinct region. Most are very linear in structure, but when all the emphasis is on the action, that's a positive — all you really need is a nice backdrop.

Onimusha stumbles slightly in that sense. Between main story segments, you get to roam freely around a kind-of open world city map which gradually expands as you progress through the game.

The problem is it just gets a bit dreary; there are only so many times you can run down the same grey and brown streets before they start to feel like filler.

And while I think Phantom Blade Zero makes you backtrack a touch too often, the region-based structure promotes a sense of adventure and travel across a more fleshed out world.

Yep, as expected, I'm having a seriously tough time trying to separate these two titles. If anything, writing this article has just made me appreciate their individual strengths even more.

Onimusha is the action connoisseur's pick, I reckon. It's the refined choice, a more nuanced but masterful take on the genre from the veterans at Capcom.

Meanwhile, Phantom Blade Zero is the blockbuster. It's bigger in scope and ambition, and its action is consistently extreme. It's arguably the more exciting release, from a relatively new studio that could become a giant of the genre.

It's a good job I've got another month or so to think this one over...

Have you played Onimusha: Way of the Sword and Phantom Blade Zero? Which one do you like best? Clash blades in the comments section below.

Robert (or Rob if you're lazy) is an assistant editor of Push Square, and has been a fan of PlayStation since the 90s, when Tekken 2 introduced him to the incredible world of video games. He still takes his fighting games seriously, but RPGs are his true passion. The Witcher, Persona, Dragon Quest, Mass Effect, Final Fantasy, Trails, Tales — he's played 'em all. A little too much, some might say.

New ID. AURA is the mainstream EV VW needs – but its software is the real story

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Volkswagen built its global empire on the promise of affordable, accessible transportation, embodied by the iconic Type 1 Beetle. Now facing an existential crisis after a decades-long push upmarket, the automaker faces a reckoning – and a retreat back into affordable, mass-market electric sedans like the new FAW-VW ID. AURA L5 (and the software that powers it) might be exactly what VW needs to reach a new generation of buyers.

Like the Ford Model T, the original VW Type 1 Beetle was an engineering-led answer to a basic problem: making family transportation affordable and accessible to the masses. And regardless of how you feel about Elon Musk, the Tesla Model 3 has become the spiritual successor to those cars, bringing electric transportation to millions of mainstream drivers. As the Model 3 approaches its 11th year in public view, the recently revealed all-electric ID. AURA L5 and VW JETTA looks like it could be a compelling rival to the mainstream M3.

Co-developed by Volkswagen’s Chinese and German design teams and set to be built by the FAW-VW JV, the ID. AURA L5 is an Accord/Camry-sized electric family car a practical fastback-style rear hatch, frameless doors, and a claimed “84.4% usable floor-area ratio.” The power-operated hatchback opens its maw more than 70 degrees, revealing a 39″ wide opening that can handle groceries, luggage, golf clubs, pets, bodies – whatever you got.

Don’t take my word for it, though. This is the original text from the company’s press release (translated from Chinese by Google):

The ID. AURA L5 is a pure electric family sedan, jointly designed by Chinese and German design teams, and highly replicates the design essence of the ID. AURA concept car shown at the 2025 Shanghai Auto Show. Its short overhang and long wheelbase proportions create a sleek and flowing coupe-like stance, achieving a “usable floor area ratio” of 84.4%. The sides feature true frameless doors, semi-concealed door handles, and an electric hatchback tailgate with an opening angle exceeding 70°, and an opening width exceeding 1000 mm, greatly improving the convenience of loading and unloading large items, deeply integrating fastback aesthetics with practicality. Some models will be equipped with LiDAR and feature the Hyper Sense 8 end-to-end large model, making daily travel easier and more convenient for customers.

The production version of the ID. AURA L5 is expected to pack the same 155 kW (~208 hp) electric motors announced when the concept AURA L5 was first shown last year (battery specs were not released), and was shown as part of a broader product announcement of what VW Group China would be showcasing at this year’s China International Import Expo (CIIE). These include the first two models fully equipped with Volkswagen Group’s locally developed VEGA “integrated technology matrix” (read: software), and the Volkswagen Anhui intelligent pure electric “coupe” crossover.

The announcement coincides with reports from outlets like Autocar and Reuters that Volkswagen is evaluating whether to deploy its China-developed electrical architecture across the majority of its European markets.

“According to internal discussions confirmed by Group board member and Volkswagen brand CEO Thomas Schäfer,” reports Autocar’s Greg Kable, “up to 75% of the firm’s core models in Europe could eventually use the China Electronic Architecture.”

The Volkswagen Group has been struggling to deliver a software experience that resonates with customers. To their credit, they’re looking for outside help, spending billions of dollars on a software collaboration with EV startup Rivian for its upcoming electric truck brand Scout rather than going it alone. With billions of dollars and potential future of the company at stake, then, this seems like the most interesting bit of the announcement:

During the event, the immersive interactive experience exhibit “Digital Magic Cube,” which made its debut at last year’s China International Import Expo, will undergo an iterative upgrade to showcase the core strength of CEA (China Electronic Architecture), the software pillar of Volkswagen Group’s integrated self-developed technology matrix VEGA, to the attendees.

To date, CEA has enabled five new models from three joint ventures within the Volkswagen Group on a large scale, and software iterations have already been completed this year. CEA brings three core advantages to the Group’s new model lineup in China: intelligent accessibility, continuous upgrades, and safety redundancy. Through immersive interactive presentations, it provides consumers with the most intuitive experience. Empowered by the new AI R&D toolchain “C-Brain,” CEA introduces AI agents into software development, design, and engineering verification, enabling more flexible and efficient integration and innovation of vehicle intelligence capabilities, giving vehicles powerful thinking and perception abilities. Intelligent vehicles powered by CEA can better understand consumer needs, more efficiently create in-vehicle intelligent application scenarios, and better enhance digital intelligent safety redundancy, bringing Chinese consumers a new generation of intelligent mobility experience defined by Volkswagen standards

Sensible looking average car which should appeal to VW buyers provided the price, charging speed and range are right. Especially like that it has a hatch without being SUV shaped.

The show runs through November 10th, and we’ll be keeping an eye out for more details on the ID. AURA lineup and the Anhui intelligent pure electric coupe, including its powertrain, battery, and software specifications, as they become available.

Frontier Hires A Wizz Air Executive As Two Leaders Walk Out

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Frontier announced Monday that Michael Delehant will join as Executive Vice President and Chief Operating Officer (COO) on November 30, 2026 according to AirlineGeeks. He most recently ran operational and commercial activities as a group Managing Director at Wizz Air, and before that he was chief strategy and network officer at Vueling; he also held several leadership roles at Southwest. He will report to Frontier CEO, Jimmy Dempsey.

The other half of the announcement is the exits. Trevor Stedke, Senior Vice President of Operations, and Alex Clerc, Senior Vice President of Customers, will both depart on November 13. Frontier didn’t provide a reason for either. Not all departures have a “spending more time with my family” boilerplate announcement provided and there’s nothing wrong with that. But  it’s telling that the person running operations and the person in charge of customers are both gone, unironically about two weeks before a new operations boss shows up for work.

James Fenner, who has spent more than a decade at Frontier and was most recently Vice president of Revenue and Loyalty, becomes Senior Vice President and Chief Revenue Officer. His job now covers pricing, revenue management, loyalty and network planning. Most of those make sense to me together with the exception of loyalty. Pricing and revenue management go hand-in-hand as does network planning. But with the Frontier getting a dedicated first class, and the loss of Spirit (so far), Allegiant/Sun Country merging, and the struggles JetBlue is facing and Frontier’s own revenue challenges – it feels like loyalty should be its own gig.

Bobby Schroeter becomes Chief Marketing Officer, overseeing the brand and customer experience. With the customers executive leaving, customer experience moves under marketing. I would have thought loyalty responsibilities would have fallen to CMO but I am apparently mistaken.

Frontier lost $104 million in the Q3 2026, compared with a $77 million loss a year earlier. Removing special items and the airline made $40 million on a record $1.36 billion in revenue. Matthew covered it the day the numbers dropped as a loss despite record revenue, and he found reason for optimism.

Dempsey took over after longtime chief, Barry Biffle, left suddenly in December last year and was named CEO in January. This CEO, on the job about 10 months, is swapping out the operations chief, the customer chief and handing the revenue chief and in essence reinventing the carrier. I want to applaud Dempsey for taking his time to evaluate before making such dramatic changes. Newly installed leaders often want their own people and to break from the past to do things their way and make their own mark. However, as long as it has taken to make these changes, many might have presumed that he felt he was able to make those changes with the current staff in place.

Hiring a Wizz Air executive indicates what Frontier thinks its future playbook is. Delehant comes from two low-cost European carriers, Wizz and Vueling, and those two airlines operate differently from each other and the market is different from Frontier. Vueling is owned by International Airlines Group, the same parent as British Airways, Iberia, LEVEL, and Aer Lingus. Vueling operates outside of the network carrier model with connections and oneworld partner feed but still benefits from the incredible buying power of IAG. Wizz does not.

But for those who have called the death of LCCs and ULCCs in the United States with the shut down of Spirit, the new Frontier could fly in the face of that. Further, as pointed out on this site before, based solely on flying people and things, Ryan Air might be the most profitable true airline (not loyalty program) in the world. It closed last year earning $2.4bn, and every carrier that placed above Ryan had a loyalty program and credit card tie-in.

One thing that has become very clear, is that first class in the United States simply must be offered to be successful. Frontier, Allegiant/Sun Country, and JetBlue have all added a first class product this year (JetBlue had Mint for long haul but its shorter haul domestic first class is genuinely new.) Spirit had just moved into this space too, separating its Big Front Seat product into a First Class cabin, then European-style Business Class (blocked middle seat) though the writing was already on the wall in Fort Lauderdale.

These changes at Frontier could foretell the future of the ultra low cost model in the United States, first class and all.

Whether Delehunt’s changes have the capability of increasing Frontier Airlines stock price will likely determine just how much further he goes. But he’s right to install his own leadership, and given the market segment, quite savvy to find executives with experience in the ULCC space and at least some overlap in the United States in Southwest Airlines. Frontier has a credit card, its loyalty division now shares a boss with revenue management, and a hub model at least in part so it’s not a like-for-like fit with Wizz or other ULCC models. But it’s not a miss either, and I am interested in seeing just how much of the Spirit vacuum Frontier chooses to operate.

AI Agents Still Need Middlemen. But Will They Need Amadeus?

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

Enter your email for a complimentary article + exclusive offers.

Photo Credit: An Amadeus office in Bad Homburg, Germany. Adobe Stock / Peter Brauers

Amadeus has the scale and airline relationships, but those advantages don't guarantee it a direct role in AI-agent bookings.

Amadeus has spent decades connecting airlines with travel sellers. AI agents may change how customers shop, but somebody still has to find flights, complete bookings, and handle changes. On its Q3 2026 earnings call on Friday, November 6, an analyst asked why Meta's Muse was using Duffel rather than Amadeus to do that work.

CFO Carol Borg said the Muse-Duffel partnership supports Amadeus' belief that AI platforms will use aggregators rather than build booking systems themselves. She doesn't think it displaces the GDS model: "It actually reinforces it."

But Duffel, not Amadeus, has the direct relationship with Muse. Other companies are pursuing similar roles across airline distribution, hotel booking and payments.

As Skift reported in September, Muse uses Duffel to access flight inv

Decision intelligence for travel leaders.

Amadeus used its Q3 2026 earnings call on November 6 to argue that AI platforms will plug into aggregators rather than build their own booking systems, a position CFO Carol Borg said the Meta Muse–Duffel partnership "reinforces." The detail behind that claim is that Duffel, not Amadeus, holds the direct relationship with Muse, even though Duffel's inventory includes direct airline connections, NDC content, and traditional GDS inventory from Travelport. A growing set of intermediaries is taking similar positions: Selfbook powers hotel booking and payments for Perplexity, Nuitée supplies hotel booking infrastructure to Grab and Revolut and is building APIs for AI applications, Katanox has partnered with Selfbook on distribution and settlement, and AirGateway and Kyte offer API-based airline connectivity. CEO Luis Maroto pointed to roughly 3 billion daily searches and said servicing complex itineraries at that scale is harder than connecting to individual airlines. The financials show pricing holding up as volumes slip: bookings fell 2.8% in the first nine months of 2026, which the company attributed largely to Middle East disruption, while revenue per booking rose 5%. Group revenue rose 5.2% at constant currency to €5.08 billion (about $5.67 billion), with adjusted EBIT up 5.5%, and management expects full-year results near the lower end of guidance while maintaining its 2026–2028 targets.

God of War Spin-Off Game Seemingly Getting Surprise PS4 Port

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

God of War: Sons of Sparta, the retro-style Metroidvania by Mega Cat Studios, is seemingly coming to the PS4.

The game first launched on the PS5 as a State of Play shadow drop earlier this year.

Word comes courtesy of the social media account PlayStation Game Size, which frequently scrapes Sony’s servers for unannounced information. It most recently accurately leaked the file size of GTA 6, for example.

Writing on X (or Twitter), it simply said: “God of War: Sons of Sparta - PS4.”

Asked for clarification about whether the spin-off side-scroller is coming to Sony’s last-gen console, it simply replied, “Yes.”

Given the style of game, I imagine Mega Cat Studios would have absolutely no problems porting this title to the PS4 – it’s just surprising it’s going to the effort to do so.

While the last-gen console remains popular, Sony has mostly moved on from it now. The last first-party game it released on the console was MLB The Show 23 in 2023, so it’s been over three years.

Should this turn out to be true, PS4 owners will be getting access to a likeable if largely unremarkable Metroidvania.

In our 7/10 God of War: Sons of Sparta review, my colleague Stephen Tailby wrote:

“God of War: Sons of Sparta is a fun spin-off that takes a series we all know and love and places it somewhere entirely different. It's a mostly successful experiment; the Metroidvania structure works well with the franchise's mechanics, and it looks and sounds the part. However, it takes too long to really come into its own, leaving the early parts of the game feeling a bit lacking compared to the richer experience found later on. If you have the patience to see it through, and can overlook some rough edges here and there, this is a worthwhile adventure for fans that delves into Kratos' history in a way we haven't seen before.”

Assuming PlayStation Game Size is accurate, I imagine an announcement will be made before the end of the year.

Missing teenager rescued after reaching out to another player in Among Us

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

"Our priority in this case was the safe return of the minor victim."

A teenager who went missing in Utah more than 17 months ago has been rescued from a home in Oregon after she got in touch with another young player in Among Us.

According to a federal criminal complaint reported by Newsweek and KUTV (via IGN), the girl, who was 15 when she disappeared from Garden City, Utah, on 29th May 2025, began chatting with another teenager in Among Us on or around 15th October this year.

The pair later moved their conversation to a different social media app, where she told them who she was and that she was being held against her will in Oregon. She had been given a phone with no cellular data, which meant she could only get online via wi-fi.

The other teen told their parent, who contacted the FBI on 1st November. Agents listened in on a video call between the parent and the girl, and she was recovered from the house later that day. She is now 17.

Adam Hunter Harmel, 39, of Huntington, Oregon, was arrested during a traffic stop.

According to the US Attorney's Office for the District of Utah, he allegedly met the girl online in May 2025, then drove to Utah to pick her up and took her back to his home across state lines. He was charged on 2nd November with transportation with intent to engage in criminal sexual activity, and made his first court appearance in Boise, Idaho, on 4th November.

"Our priority in this case was the safe return of the minor victim," said US Attorney Melissa Holyoak. "Those who allegedly exploit and prey upon the most vulnerable members of our communities will be relentlessly investigated and held accountable."

Utah's US Attorney's Office stresses that a complaint is merely an allegation, and that all defendants are presumed innocent until proven guilty in a court of law.

Following Assassin's Creed Shadows' and Assassin's Creed Black Flag Resynced's success, Ubisoft is apparently struggling to strike while the iron's hot. Its next mainline game in the series - codenamed Assassin's Creed Hexe - has reportedly been delayed internally, while the publisher works on two other AC games.

AC Hexe was actually announced alongside Shadows more than four years ago, though we haven't had a concrete look at the supposedly witchy, spooky open-world game since. All we knew for sure is that Hexe would launch sometime after Shadows, and here we are now.

Insider Gaming now reports that Assassin's Creed Hexe ran into further development road bumps and has since been internally delayed by another year, citing anonymous sources in the know. According to the report, the likelier release date for Hexe could be sometime in 2028.

AC Hexe hasn't had the smoothest journey to launch so far, with or without another internal delay. The game's original creative director Clint Hocking left the company earlier this year, with Black Flag and Origins' lead developer Jean Guesdon taking over for him. Two months later, game director Benoit Richer also departed.

Still, Ubisoft's purportedly cooking up more than one dish in its historical and historically stealthy-stabby series. Well connected YouTuber j0nathan recently claimed there are two other codenamed AC games in development - Assassin's Creed Mars and Assassin's Creed Pocket.

AC Mars supposedly takes the series to Ancient Rome for the first time, perhaps tying into the upcoming Netflix show in some way. Assassin's Creed Brotherhood did take place in Rome during the Italian Renaissance era, so there's probably fun ways for the lore to connect if Ubisoft did ever parkour down this route. On the other hand, Pocket is apparently set in Jerusalem and is said to be a mobile game, hence the name. As always, take any rumors with a handful of salt until Ubisoft confirms plans for itself.

So, there's lots of Assassin's Creed flying about the place. To keep up with everything going on, check out every upcoming Assassin's Creed game currently in development.

World of Warcraft: Forever is the biggest launch in the MMO's history, pulling in more concurrent players than any previous WoW launch.

The game hasn't just tempted back lapsed players. In a follow-up post, executive producer and World of Warcraft vice president Holly Longdale said it brought in the most new players for a WoW launch in 15 years.

"Welcome all! We're so happy you're here experiencing Azeroth for the first time alongside those of us playing it for the first time... again," she wrote.

“He’s Got The Yips” Mads Hermansen’s West Ham Nightmare Goes Beyond Bad Form

📅 2026-11-08  |  Read Source →

There is a phenomenon in sport known as the yips. It’s a condition which seems to affect previously capable sportsmen, psychologically blocking them from performing even the simplest of tasks. In darts, it can become difficult to release the dart from the hand, while in golf it can make putting almost impossible.

In essence, you’re frozen, seemingly unable to perform a task which should have become second nature. And it was during Blackburn Rovers’ second goal against West Ham yesterday, when I watched Mads Hermansen fail to dive, that I found myself wondering whether this goalkeeper has developed the footballing equivalent of the yips.

It wasn’t just that goal, of course. The first was equally concerning, when Ezra Mayers was beaten down the flank and the ball was crossed into the penalty area. The situation required some kind of intervention from Hermansen, but there was none. No punch, no parry, no save. Just indecision and a complete lack of intervention.

Unfortunately, this apparent hesitation has permeated Hermansen’s game recently and it’s hard not to conclude that he’s got the yips. There have been occasions when he has looked like little more than a bystander as goals have flown past him, sometimes appearing rooted to the spot or simply falling over rather than making a convincing attempt to save the ball.

The statistics are equally worrying. Based on the match figures we’ve examined, Hermansen has saved just 20% of the shots on target he has faced over West Ham’s last three Championship matches, conceding eight goals from ten attempts on target.

That’s an extraordinary return for a goalkeeper at any level, let alone one playing for a club with West Ham’s promotion ambitions. And while defenders must accept their share of the responsibility, the goalkeeper ultimately has to provide some resistance when opponents get a shot away.

The warning signs were already there before Cardiff, and his latest performance has only increased my concerns. I don’t know whether Hermansen is suffering from anything resembling the yips, but from watching him, I do wonder whether his confidence has deteriorated to the point where instinctive reactions are being replaced by hesitation.

This has become a huge problem, and one Scott Parker needs to address immediately. As things stand, I would have more confidence in either Alphonse Areola or Finlay Herrick than a goalkeeper who currently appears unable to make the saves West Ham desperately need.

Areola is an experienced international goalkeeper whom Parker already knows from their time together at Fulham. I thought he deserved his opportunity against Blackburn, and nothing I witnessed yesterday has changed my mind.

Hermansen’s performance was among the biggest disappointments in our Blackburn player ratings, and continuing to select him simply because he was previously considered first choice doesn’t make much sense when his performances have deteriorated so badly.

Clearly, there is a bigger issue to address in January, when West Ham will have another opportunity to examine their goalkeeping options. But we’ve only just entered November, and there are still two months and a hell of a lot of fixtures to negotiate before the transfer window opens.

That’s an awful lot of potential points to lose while waiting for a goalkeeper to rediscover his confidence. Parker has an international break in which to make some important decisions, and I would suggest the identity of his first-choice goalkeeper should be very near the top of that list.

Kris Gonzo (commonly known to the West Ham community as Gonzo) is the co-founder of Hammers Chat (established 2014) and the lead video content creator and columnist for Claret and Hugh.

With over a decade of professional sports media experience, he is a frequent West Ham United analyst and contributor for Sky Sports News, BBC Football Focus, and BBC Radio 5 Live. He previously served as an official video and content creator for West Ham United FC during the club's tenure at Upton Park and featured in the official documentary commemorating the historic final game at the Boleyn Ground.

A lifelong Hammer since the legendary 10-0 victory over Bury, when he isn't providing daily match analysis on the Hammers Chat YouTube Channel or broadcasting on X (@GONZObignose), he can be found walking his dog and restoring classic pinball machines.

Needs to be dropped and keeper, forward and centre half brought in during the Jan transfer window. That’s the only hope for promotion we have in my view, unless Scott can pull a rabbit or two out of the hat!

Cannot believe how this problem still goes on, with all the professionals looking at what goes on in training and behind the scenes, whoever saw something in our keeper which was enough to cost our club 20 mil, and now your labelling him with a condition or letters what I don’t understand. I have posted many times about his form and the word or description of his efforts that I would use is certainly 4 letters but begins s and ends t. He is a constant moaner gives his defence no positive verbal, cannot catch a cold, why pass back to him so many times, even directly from k/off, he cannot distribute the football accurately. I think the next fixture presents the perfect opportunity to see what Herrick’s potential looks like.
